If a base rule does not apply, then we attempt to match resources from both branches, and then call the algorithm recursively on smaller symbolic heaps with some resources moved into the returned anti-frame.

Ls-Start-L
applies when both branches contain the same resource
e_{1}
; however, one includes
e_{1}
as the head of a list segment and the other refers to
e_{1}
using a points-to predicate. The points-to predicate must be the head of the list, so we move it into
M
and recurse on the tail of the list.
The
\mapsto
-Match
rule applies when both branches use
e_{1}
in a points-to predicate, therefore the values pointed to must be equal too.

Ls-End-L
applies when both branches have list segments starting at the same address, so one segment must be a prefix of the other.

As in
Calcagno et al. 2009
, we do not consider cases where pointers are aliased. For example, if the two branches are
x\mapsto 1
and
y\mapsto 1
, then it is possible that
x=y
. Precluding this solution helps limit the number of options we consider.
Calcagno et al. 2009
remark that this loss of precision is not detrimental in practice.

Resource Adding.

Adding resources that are only present on one side is the last resort, since it involves checking a potentially expensive side condition of the form
\Pi\land\Sigma\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\not\vdash\mathsf{false}
. The
Missing-L
rule handles the case wherein one branch refers to resources not present in the other. This is different from the
Base-True-L
rule, since it handles cases where
both
branches refer to resources not explicitly present in the other. For example,
Missing-L
can solve
x\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{true}\lhd[\textbf{?}]\rhd y\mapsto Y\ast\mathsf{true}
even though
the
Base-True
rules do not apply.
If one side of the judgement contains a list segment, but the other side does
not
contain the spatial assertion
\mathsf{true}
, then there is a possible solution where the list segment is empty.

Emp-Ls-L
handles such cases by forcing the list segment to be empty.

As we mentioned at the beginning of the section, the tri-abduction algorithm follows a similar structure to that of bi-abduction
(
Calcagno et al. 2011
, Algorithm 3)
.

\mathsf{triab}(P,Q)=\{(M,F_{1},F_{2})\mid M\in\textsf{abduce-par}(P\ast\mathsf{true},Q\ast\mathsf{true}),\;M\vdash P\ast F_{1},\;M\vdash Q\ast F_{2}\}

We first abduce a set of anti-frames using
Algorithm

1
such that
M\vDash P\ast\mathsf{true}
and
M\vDash Q\ast\mathsf{true}
for each
M
. Adding the spatial assertion
\mathsf{true}
absorbs extra resources; if
P
and
Q
have different memory footprints, then there is no
M
such that
P\lhd[M]\rhd Q
, but adding
\mathsf{true}
to both sides of the entailments allows
M
to refer to resources present in only one branch.
Next, we use the frame inference procedure from
Berdine
et al. 2005b
to find
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
such that
M\vDash P\ast F_{1}
and
M\vDash Q\ast F_{2}
. Applying frame inference is necessary because
M
may mention resources present in
P
, but not
Q
(and vice versa). The set of solutions
is valid according to the following correctness result, which follows from the soundness of the proof system in
Berdine
et al. 2005b
.

Theorem 5.1 (Tri-abduction).

If
(M,F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}(P,Q)
, then
M\vDash P\ast F_{1}
and
M\vDash Q\ast F_{2}

5.2.
Symbolic Execution Algorithm

The algorithm is presented as a symbolic execution, which computes an abstract semantics for a program—denoted
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
—represented as a set of pairs of pre- and postconditions. The precondition is a single symbolic heap
P
, whereas the postcondition is an outcome assertion
\varphi
. The parameter
T
is a lookup table that gets updated with summaries for procedures as the analysis moves through the codebase. Intuitively, we think about specifications as starting in a single state and producing a collection of outcomes, as the execution may branch due to effects. Abductor operates in a similar fashion, but the postcondition is a disjunction rather than an outcome conjunction. The intended semantics is captured by the following soundness result.

Theorem 5.2 (Symbolic Execution Soundness).

If
(P,\varphi)\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
, then

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\rangle}

The strategy for the analysis is to accumulate a set of outcomes while moving forward through the program. At each step, every outcome in the current summary must be sequenced with a summary for the next command using bi-abduction. This is achieved using the
seq
procedure, defined in
Figure

4
, which takes in an outcome assertion
\varphi
, a set of summaries for the next command
C
, and
\vec{x}
, the variables modified by
C
. It computes a set of missing anti-frames
M
and postconditions
\psi
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
is a valid specification for
C
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{seq}(\makebox[12.6112pt][c]{$\top$},S,\vec{x})=~

\displaystyle\{(\mathsf{emp},\top)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{seq}(\makebox[34.06143pt][c]{$\varphi_{1}\bowtie\varphi_{2}$},S,\vec{x})=~

\displaystyle\hskip-68.00098pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\big\{(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1}\bowtie\psi^{\prime}_{2})\mid&(M_{1},\psi_{1})\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi_{1},S,\vec{x})\\
&(M_{2},\psi_{2})\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi_{2},S,\vec{x})\\
&(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1},\psi^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})\big\}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mathsf{seq}(\makebox[34.06143pt][c]{$\varphi^{(a)}$},S,\vec{x})=~

\displaystyle\{(M,\psi^{(a)})\mid(M,\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,S,\vec{x})\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{seq}(\makebox[34.06143pt][c]{$\mathsf{ok}:P$},S,\vec{x})=~

\displaystyle\hskip-68.00098pt\big\{(M,\psi^{\prime})\mid(Q,\psi)\in S,(M,\psi^{\prime})\in\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}(P,Q,\psi,\vec{x})\big\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{seq}(\makebox[34.06143pt][c]{$\mathsf{er}:Q$},S,\vec{x})=~

\displaystyle\{(\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{er}:Q)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}(\exists\vec{Z}.\Delta,Q,\psi,\vec{x})=

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{l}\big\{(M^{\prime},(\psi\circledast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}])\\
\mid(M,F)\in\mathsf{biab}(\Delta,Q)\\
\hskip 8.50012pt(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})=\mathsf{rename}(\Delta,M,Q,\{\psi\},\vec{X},\vec{x})\big\}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(P_{1},P_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})=

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{array}[]{l}\big\{(M^{\prime},(\psi_{1}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{1}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}],\\
\phantom{\big\{(M^{\prime},\,}(\psi_{2}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{2}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}])\\
~\mid(M,F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}(P_{1},P_{2})\\
\hskip 8.50012pt(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})=\mathsf{rename}(\mathsf{emp},M,\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\},\emptyset,\vec{x})\big\}\end{array}

Figure 4.
Sequencing procedure. The vector
\vec{X}
is assumed to be fresh and the same size as
\vec{x}
, and
\mathord{\bowtie}\in\{\vee,\oplus\}
.

Sequencing after
\top
and
(\mathsf{er}:Q)
has no effect, since
\top
carries no information about the current branch, and
(\mathsf{er}:Q)
means the program has crashed. Sequencing after
\varphi^{(a)}
simply sequences
\varphi
and then reapplies the weight. Sequential composition is implemented in the
(\mathsf{ok}:P)
case, where bi-abduction is used to reconcile the current outcome with each summary for the next command. The
\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}
procedure is similar to
AbduceAndAdapt
from
Calcagno et al. 2011
, in which a renaming step is applied to ensure that the anti-frame
M
is not phrased in terms of any program variables, therefore meeting the side condition of the frame rule. The details of renaming and why it is required for correctness are explained in
Appendix

E
.

Our algorithm differs from Abductor in the cases with multiple program branches. This is where we use tri-abduction to obtain a precondition that is guaranteed to be valid for all program paths, allowing us to analyze the program in a single pass (unlike Abductor, which must re-evaluate the program using each candidate precondition). After sequencing each of the outcomes in the precondition
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
with the next command, we use
\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}
to obtain the single renamed anti-frame
M
that is safe for both branches.
The soundness property for
seq
is stated below.

Lemma 5.3 (Seq).

If
(M,\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,S,\vec{x})
,
\vec{x}=\mathsf{mod}(C)
, and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\vartheta\rangle}
for all
(P,\vartheta)\in S
, then
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
.

Symbolic Execution Algorithm.

The core symbolic execution algorithm, shown in
Figure

5
, computes a
local
symbolic semantics which can be augmented using the frame rule to obtain summaries in larger heaps. For example, the semantics for
\mathsf{skip}
is simply the triple
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp}\rangle}~\mathsf{skip}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp}\rangle}
, but this implies that running the program in any heap will yield that same heap in the end.
Executing
C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}
uses
\mathsf{seq}
; summaries for
C_{1}
are sequenced with all the summaries for
C_{2}
. Choices
C_{1}+C_{2}
are analyzed by computing summaries for each path and reconciling them with tri-abduction.

We split
\mathsf{assume}~e
into two cases: if
e
is a simple test (equality or inequality), then we generate two specifications with the precondition stating that
e
is true and the postcondition being unchanged, or
e
being false and the outcome being eliminated. This is similar to the
“assume-as-assert”
behavior of Abductor and produces precise specifications without a priori knowledge of the logical conditions that will occur.
If
e
is a weight literal
a
, then we weight the current outcome by it. Other expressions are not supported due to limitations of the bi-abduction solver of
Calcagno et al. 2009
, which we also use.
Similarly,
while
loops use a least fixed point to unroll the loop, and produce one summary for each possible number of iterations. We will see more options for analyzing loops later on.

The abstract semantics of atomic actions mostly follow the small axioms of
O’Hearn
et al. 2001
, with failure cases inspired by Incorrectness Separation Logic
(
Raad et al. 2020
)
. Each memory operation has three specifications: one in which the pointer is allocated and the operation accordingly succeeds, and two failure cases where the pointer is not allocated or null. Procedure calls rely on pre-computed summaries in a lookup table
T
, which is a parameter to
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}
.

\begin{array}[]{l|l}C\in\mathsf{Cmd}&\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{skip}&\{(\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp})\}\\
C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}&\{(P\ast M,\psi)\mid(P,\varphi)\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),(M,\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),\mathsf{mod}(C_{2}))\}\\
C_{1}+C_{2}&\begin{array}[]{ll}\{(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\psi^{\prime}_{2})\mid&(M_{1},\psi_{1})\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),(M_{2},\psi_{2})\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),\\
&(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1},\psi^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\mathsf{mod}(C_{1},C_{2}))\}\end{array}\\
\mathsf{assume}~b&\{(b\land\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:b\land\mathsf{emp}),(\lnot b\land\mathsf{emp},\top^{(\dsser{0})}\}\\
\mathsf{assume}~a&\{(\mathsf{emp},(\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp})^{(a)}\}\\
\mathsf{while}~b~\mathsf{do}~C&\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{lfp}~S.&\{(\lnot b\land\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:\lnot b\land\mathsf{emp})\}\mathop{\cup}\\
&\{(M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land b,\psi)\\
&\mid(M_{1},\varphi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\mathsf{ok}:b\land\mathsf{emp},\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),\mathsf{mod}(C)),(M_{2},\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,S,\mathsf{mod}(C))\}\end{array}\\
\\
c\in\mathsf{Act}&\left\llbracket c\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr x\coloneqq e&\{(x=X\land\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:x=e[X/x]\land\mathsf{emp})\}\\
x\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}()&\{(x=X\land\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:\exists Y.x\mapsto Y)\}\\
\mathsf{free}(e)&\{(e\mapsto X,\mathsf{ok}:e\not\mapsto),(e\not\mapsto,\mathsf{er}:e\not\mapsto),(\mathsf{emp}\land e=\mathsf{null},\mathsf{er}:\mathsf{emp}\land e=\mathsf{null})\}\\
{[e_{1}]\leftarrow e_{2}}&\{(e_{1}\mapsto X,\mathsf{ok}:e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}),(e_{1}\not\mapsto,\mathsf{er}:e_{1}\not\mapsto),(\mathsf{emp}\land e_{1}=\mathsf{null},\mathsf{er}:\mathsf{emp}\land e_{1}=\mathsf{null})\}\\
x\leftarrow[e]&\{(x=X\!\land\!e\mapsto Y,\mathsf{ok}:x=Y\!\land\!e[X/x]\mapsto Y),\!(e\not\mapsto,\mathsf{er}\!:\!e\not\mapsto),\!(\mathsf{emp}\!\land\!e\!=\!\mathsf{null},\!\mathsf{er}\!:\!\mathsf{emp}\!\land\!e\!=\!\mathsf{null})\}\\
\mathsf{error}()&\{(\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{er}:\mathsf{emp})\}\\
f(\vec{e})&\{(P\land\vec{x}=\vec{X},\varphi)\mid(P,\varphi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\mathsf{ok}:\vec{x}=\vec{e}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]\land\mathsf{emp},T(f(\vec{x})),\mathsf{mod}(f))\})\}\end{array}

Figure 5.
Symbolic execution of commands and actions, all logical variables
X,Y\in\mathsf{LVar}
are assumed to be fresh,
a\in A
is a program weight, and
b\Coloneqq e_{1}=e_{2}\mid e_{1}\neq e_{2}
is a simple test.

Simulating Pulse
. As recounted by
O’Hearn 2020
;
Raad et al. 2020
;
Le et al. 2022
, the scalability of IL-based analyses stems from their ability to
drop disjuncts
. Analyzers such as Abductor accumulate a disjunction of possible end states at each program point. For incorrectness, it is not necessary to remember
all
of these possible states;
any
of them leading to an error constitutes a bug.
Since IL allows
strengthening
of postconditions, those disjuncts can be soundly dropped.

We take a slightly different view, which nonetheless enables us to drop
paths
in the same way. We differentiate between program choices that result from logical conditions (
i.e.,
if and while statements) vs computational effects (
i.e.,
nondeterministic or probabilistic choice). In the former cases, we generate multiple summaries in order to precisely keep track of which initial states will result in which outcomes. In the latter case, we use an outcome conjunction rather than a disjunction to join the outcomes. While we cannot drop outcomes per se, we can replace them by
\top
, ensuring that they will not be explored any further according to the definition of
seq
(
Figure

4
).
This can be implemented by altering the definition of choice as follows.

\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)=\{(P,\varphi\oplus\top)\mid(P,\varphi)\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)\}\cup\{(P,\varphi\oplus\top)\mid(P,\varphi)\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)\}

Given this modification, the algorithm remains sound with respect to the same semantics (
i.e.,

Theorem

5.2
), but it no longer fits with the spirit of correctness reasoning, since some of the program outcomes are left unspecified. We will see in
Section

6.1
how it can be used for efficient bug-finding.

Now, we have a situation where each element of
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
stands alone as a sound summary for the program
C
, and represents a particular trace. This corresponds to taking a particular logical branch in if statements, unfolding while loops for a fixed number of iterations, and specific (nondeterministic or probabilistic) choices.
So, eliminating elements from the set will only preclude possible summaries without affecting the correctness of the existing ones, meaning that we can drop paths by,
e.g.,
unrolling loops for a certain number of iterations and only taking one path when encountering a
+
.

Le et al. 2022
refer to this as depth and width of the analysis, respectively.

Loop invariants and partial correctness.

An alternative to bounded unrolling for deterministic and non-deterministic programs (but not probabilistic ones) is to use loop invariants.
We achieve this by altering the rule for while loops to the following.

\left\llbracket\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)=\{(I,(\mathsf{ok}:I\land\lnot e)\vee\top^{(\dsser{0})}\mid(I\land e,\mathsf{ok}:I)\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)\}

The truth of the invariant
I
is preserved by the loop body, therefore it must remain true
if
the loop exits. However, the invariant cannot guarantee that the loop terminates, meaning that we lose the typical reachability guarantees of outcome logic and must use a weaker
partial correctness
specification.
The possibility of nontermination is expressed by the disjunction with
\top^{(\dsser{0})}
—either
I\land\lnot e
holds, or the program diverges and there are no outcomes. Since reachability cannot be guaranteed, loop invariants are not suitable for true positive bug-finding, in which errors must be witnessed by an actual trace.

Finding loop invariants is generally undecidable, however techniques from abstract interpretation
(
Cousot and Cousot 1977
)
can be used to find invariants by framing the problem as a fixed point computation over a finite domain, thereby guaranteeing convergence. This is the approach taken in Abductor
(
Calcagno et al. 2011
)
, which uses the same symbolic heaps, but without outcome conjunctions. In nondeterministic programs, we can convert outcome conjunctions into disjunctions since
(\mathsf{ok}:P)\oplus(\mathsf{ok}:Q)\Rightarrow(\mathsf{ok}:P\vee Q)
. We therefore speculate that we can use the same technique as Abductor, although we leave a complete exploration of this idea to future work.

Nondeterministic Allocation
. Memory bugs can arise in C from failing to check whether the address returned by
malloc
is non-null. This is often modeled using nondeterminism, wherein the semantics of
malloc
returns either a valid pointer or null, nondeterministically. Our language is generic over effects, so we do not have a nondeterministic
malloc
operation, but we can add
x\coloneqq\mathsf{malloc}()
as syntactic sugar for
(x\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}())+(x\coloneqq\mathsf{null})
, and derive the following semantics:

\left\llbracket x\coloneqq\mathsf{malloc}()\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)=\{(x=X\land\mathsf{emp},(\mathsf{ok}:x=\mathsf{null}\land\mathsf{emp})\oplus(\mathsf{ok}:\exists Y.x\mapsto Y))\}

Reusing Summaries.

Though partial correctness specifications are incompatible with bug-finding, and under-approximate specifications are incompatible with verification, there is still overlap in summaries that can be used for both. Many procedures in a given codebase do not include loops or branching, so their summaries are equally valid for both correctness and incorrectness, and also in programs with different interpretations of choice.
In other cases, when a procedure does have multiple outcomes, it is easy to convert a correctness specification into several individual incorrectness ones, since the following implication is sound. We will see this in action in
Section

6.1
.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{1}\oplus\psi_{2}\rangle}\qquad\implies\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{1}\oplus\top\rangle}\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{2}\oplus\top\rangle}

6.
Case Studies

We will now demonstrate how the symbolic execution algorithms work by examining two case studies, which show the applicability in both nondeterministic and probabilistic execution models.

6.1.
Nondeterministic Vector Reallocation

Our first case study involves a common error in C++ when using the
std::vector
library in which a call to
push_back
may reallocate the vector’s underlying memory buffer, invalidating any pointers to that cell that existed before the call. This was also used as a motivating example for Incorrectness Separation Logic
(
Raad et al. 2020
)
. Following their lead, we model the vector as a single pointer and we treat reallocation as nondeterministic.
The program is shown below.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{main}():\\
\quad x\leftarrow[v]\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{push\_back}(v)\fatsemi\\
\quad{[x]}\leftarrow 1\end{array}&\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{push\_back}(v):\\
\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{array}[]{lll}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}y\leftarrow[v]\fatsemi\\
\textsf{free}(y)\fatsemi\\
y\coloneqq\textsf{alloc}()\fatsemi\\
{[v]}\leftarrow y\end{array}\right)&+&\mathsf{skip}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Before we analyze the
main
procedure, we must store
\left\llbracket\mathsf{push\_back}(v)\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
in the procedure table. Since
push_back
is a common library function, it makes sense to compute summaries describing all the outcomes, which will be reusable for both correctness and incorrectness analyses. The first step is to analyze the two nondeterministic branches, which are both simple sequential programs.

\displaystyle\small\left\llbracket\begin{array}[]{l}y\leftarrow[v]\fatsemi\\
\textsf{free}(y)\fatsemi\\
y\coloneqq\textsf{alloc}()\fatsemi\\
{[v]}\leftarrow y\end{array}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}\!\!\!\!(T)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-,&\mathsf{ok}:\exists B.v\mapsto B\ast B\mapsto-\ast A\not\mapsto&)\\
(v\mapsto A\ast A\not\mapsto,&\mathsf{er}:v\mapsto A\ast A\not\mapsto&)\\
(v\not\mapsto,&\mathsf{er}:v\not\mapsto&)\\
&\cdots\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}\!(T)

\displaystyle=\large\{(\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp})\large\}

Now, we can compose the two program branches using tri-abduction. Choosing the first summary for the first branch, we get the following tri-abduction solution.

v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-\ast[\mathsf{emp}]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}[v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-]\vDash\mathsf{emp}\ast[v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-]

So, by framing
\mathsf{emp}
into the first branch and
v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-
into the second branch, we get a summary for
push_back
as a whole. This can similarly be done for the other summaries of the first branch, yielding the lookup table below.

{\small\begin{array}[]{ll}T=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllclll}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rangle}&\mathsf{push\_back}(v)&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{ok}:\exists B.v\mapsto B\ast B\mapsto-\ast{A\not\mapsto}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25})\oplus(\mathsf{ok}:v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25})\rangle}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}v\mapsto A\ast{A\not\mapsto}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rangle}&\mathsf{push\_back}(v)&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{er}:v\mapsto A\ast{A\not\mapsto}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25})\oplus(\mathsf{ok}:v\mapsto A\ast{A\not\mapsto}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25})\rangle}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{v\not\mapsto}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\rangle}&\mathsf{push\_back}(v)&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{er}:{v\not\mapsto}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25})\oplus(\mathsf{ok}:{v\not\mapsto}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25})\rangle}\\
&&&\cdots\end{array}\right\}\end{array}}

The first summary tells us that
push_back
may reallocate the underlying buffer, in which case the original pointer
A
will become deallocated. The next two summaries describe ways in which
push_back
itself can fail. We will focus on using the first summary to show how
main
will fail if the buffer gets reallocated.
We analyze
main
in an under-approximate fashion in order to look for bugs. The first step is to compute summaries for the first two commands of main. The load on the first line has three summaries according to
Figure

5
, we select the first one in which
v
is allocated.

\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:x=X\land v\mapsto Y\rangle}~x\leftarrow[v]~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:x=Y\land v\mapsto Y\rangle}\in\left\llbracket x\leftarrow[v]\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)

The procedure call on the second line requires us to look up summaries in
T
. We select the first one, but we will use an under-approximate version of it so as to explore only one of the paths

\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-\rangle}~\mathsf{push\_back}(v)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{ok}:\exists B.v\mapsto B\ast B\mapsto-\ast{A\not\mapsto})\oplus\top\rangle}

Now, we use
seq
to sequentially compose these summaries, which involves bi-abducing the postcondition of
x\leftarrow[v]
with the precondition of
\mathsf{push\_back}(v)
.

x=Y\land v\mapsto Y\ast[A=Y\ast x\mapsto-]\vDash v\mapsto A\ast A\mapsto-\ast[\mathsf{emp}]

So, after renaming, we get the following summary for the composed program:

\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:v\mapsto x\ast x\mapsto-\rangle}~x\leftarrow[v]\fatsemi\mathsf{push\_back}(v)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{ok}:\exists B.v\mapsto B\ast B\mapsto-\ast{x\not\mapsto})\oplus\top\rangle}

Now, observe that the postcondition above is only compatible with one of the summaries in
Figure

5
for the last line of the program. Since
x
is deallocated in the only specified outcome, the write into
x
must fail.
Using bi-abduction again, we can construct the following description of the error.

\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:v\mapsto x\ast x\mapsto-\rangle}~x\leftarrow[v]\fatsemi\mathsf{push\_back}(v)\fatsemi[x]\leftarrow 1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{er}:\exists B.v\mapsto B\ast B\mapsto-\ast{x\not\mapsto})\oplus\top\rangle}

6.2.
Consensus in Distributed Computing

In this case study, we use OSL to lower bound reliability rates of a distributed system. In the basic consensus algorithm, shown below,
each of three processes broadcasts a value
v_{i}
by storing it in a pointer
p_{i}
, and consensus is reached if any two of these processes broadcasted the same value. To model unreliability of the network, the broadcast procedure fails with probability 1%. We would like to know how likely we are to reach consensus, given that two of the processes agree.

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{main}():\\
\quad p_{1}\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}()\fatsemi\mathsf{broadcast}(v_{1},p_{1})\fatsemi\\
\quad p_{2}\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}()\fatsemi\mathsf{broadcast}(v_{2},p_{2})\fatsemi\\
\quad p_{3}\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}()\fatsemi\mathsf{broadcast}(v_{3},p_{3})\fatsemi\\
\quad v\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}()\fatsemi\\
\quad\mathsf{decide}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},v)\\
\\
\mathsf{broadcast}(v,p):\\
\quad\left([p]\leftarrow v\right)\oplus_{0.99}\mathsf{error}()\end{array}\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{decide}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},v):\\
\quad x_{1}\leftarrow[p_{1}]\fatsemi x_{2}\leftarrow[p_{2}]\fatsemi x_{3}\leftarrow[p_{3}]\fatsemi\\
\quad\mathsf{if}~x_{1}=x_{2}~\mathsf{then}\\
\quad\quad[v]\leftarrow x_{1}\\
\quad\mathsf{else\ if}~x_{1}=x_{3}~\mathsf{then}\\
\quad\quad[v]\leftarrow x_{1}\\
\quad\mathsf{else\ if}~x_{2}=x_{3}~\mathsf{then}\\
\quad\quad[v]\leftarrow x_{2}\\
\quad\mathsf{else}~\mathsf{skip}\end{array}

We begin by examining the summary table. The
broadcast
procedure has two outcomes corresponding to whether or not the communication went through. Though there are many summaries for
decide
, we show only the one in which the values sent on
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are equal.

\small T=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:p\mapsto-\land v=V\rangle}~\mathsf{broadcast}(v,p)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{ok}:p\mapsto V\land v=V)\oplus_{0.99}(\mathsf{er}:p\mapsto-\land v=V)\rangle}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:p_{1}\mapsto V_{1}\ast p_{2}\mapsto V_{2}\ast p_{3}\mapsto V_{3}\ast v\mapsto-\land V_{1}=V_{2}\rangle}~\mathsf{decide}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},v)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:v\mapsto V_{1}\ast\cdots\rangle}\\
\cdots\end{array}\right\}

We again use the single-path algorithm to analyze
main
, but this time we are interested only in the successfully terminating cases. We get the following summaries for each of the first three lines.

{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:v_{i}=V_{i}\rangle}~p_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}()\fatsemi\mathsf{broadcast}(v_{i},p_{i})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{ok}:v_{i}=V_{i}\land p_{i}\mapsto V_{i})\oplus_{0.99}\top\rangle}}

These three summaries can be combined—along with the simplification that
(\varphi\oplus_{a}\top)\oplus_{b}\top
implies
\varphi\oplus_{a\cdot b}\top
—to obtain the following
assertion just before the call to
decide

(\mathsf{ok}:v_{1}=V_{1}\land v_{2}=V_{2}\land v_{3}=V_{3}\land p_{1}\mapsto V_{1}\ast p_{2}\mapsto V_{2}\ast p_{3}\mapsto V_{3})\oplus_{0.99^{3}}\top

Now, we can bi-abduce the first outcome above with the precondition for
decide
shown in
T
. This sends
V_{1}=V_{2}
backwards into the precondition, and we get an overall summary for
main
telling us that if
v_{1}=v_{2}
, then the protocol will reach consensus (
v\mapsto V_{1}
) with probability
at least
97%.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:v_{1}=V_{1}\land v_{2}=V_{2}\land v_{3}=V_{3}\land V_{1}=V_{2}\rangle}~\mathsf{main}()~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{ok}:v\mapsto V_{1}\ast\cdots)\oplus_{0.9703}\top\rangle}

7.
Related Work

Separation Logic and the Frame Rule.
While many variants of separation logic exist, OSL is the only one that supports alternative effects as well as both may and must properties. The soundness of the frame rule in both partial and total correctness separation logic relies on nondeterminism and
must
properties
(
Yang and O’Hearn 2002
;
Calcagno
et al. 2007
)
, so those logics are suitable only for correctness in nondeterministic languages. Some work has been done to drop the nondeterminism requirement, for example
Baktiev 2006
proved that the frame rule is sound in a deterministic language if heap assertions are unaffected by address permutation, however this requires languages without address arithmetic.
Similarly,
Tatsuta
et al. 2009
created a deterministic separation logic, but the frame rule only applies to programs that do not allocate memory.

Incorrectness Separation Logic (ISL) has a frame rule that is compatible with may properties, but not must properties
(
Raad et al. 2020
)
. Unlike OSL, ISL has no restrictions on assertions about memory allocation; it is possible to prove that a particular address
\ell
is returned. If, after applying the frame rule,
\ell
is already in use, then the postcondition of the ISL triple becomes false, making the triple vacuous (whereas in regular separation logic and OSL, a false
pre
condition makes triples vacuous). However, whereas ISL has slightly more power to express may properties, ISL cannot express must (
i.e.,
correctness) properties and is also specialized only to nondeterminism.

Exact Separation Logic (ESL)
(
Maksimović et al. 2023
)
combines the semantics of SL and ISL to create a logic that is suitable for both correctness and incorrectness, while inheriting the limitations of both. That is, ESL can only express properties that are
both
may and must properties, meaning that it cannot under-approximate by dropping paths or over-approximate using loop invariants.

Higher-order separation logics
(
Birkedal and Yang 2007
;
Birkedal et al. 2008
)
including Iris
(
Jung et al. 2015
)
bake the frame rule into the definition of the logic’s triples, making the proof of soundness of the frame rule trivial without any additional assumptions. More precisely, triples
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\{P\}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\{Q\}}
are valid iff for any frame
F
, if
\sigma\vDash P\ast F
, then
\tau\vDash Q\ast F
for all
\tau\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
. As a tradeoff, frame baking introduces additional proof obligations whenever constructing a triple (
i.e.,
it must be shown that every inference rule is
frame preserving
). We could have used this approach in OSL, but there is no free lunch; it would have involved moving the inductive cases of
Lemma

C.7
into
Theorem

5.2
.

We chose not to do this,
as we preferred to keep the proof of the frame rule self contained. The choice is mostly orthogonal to the power of the logic, and somewhat philosophical: we proved that programs are local
actions
whereas the frame baking approach shows that you can only
express
local properties about commands. The exception to this is memory allocation, which is not a local action, so we baked the expressivity limitation into the triple semantics. We felt this was a good tradeoff, since we baked in a weaker property, which was just strong enough to complete the proof.

In draft papers published after our work on Outcome Separation Logic, Sufficient Incorrectness Logic (SIL)
(
Ascari
et al. 2023
)
and Backwards Under-Approximate triples (BUA)
(
Raad
et al. 2023
)
provide frame rules for triples based on
may
properties. In both cases, the soundness result is a bit weaker than our own (
Theorem

4.6
). That is, whereas we only require in the premise that the triple
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
is
semantically
valid,
Ascari
et al. 2023
;
Raad
et al. 2023
require that it is
derivable
. While this trick allows them to avoid baking the allocation restriction into the triple validity, it means that the soundness of the frame rule depends on the other inference rules in the proof system—a property that we wished to avoid. In addition, the inference rules for allocation in those logics are not tight, meaning that the logics are incomplete. Adding a tighter rule (with the ability to witness
which
address was nondeterministically chosen) would make the frame rule unsound, meaning that completeness of those logics is incompatible with the frame rule.

Probabilistic Separation Logics.

While some work has been done to incorporate probabilistic reasoning into separation logic, these logics differ from OSL in the scope and applicability of the frame rule, and have not been shown to be compatible with bi-abduction.
Quantitative Separation Logic
(
Batz et al. 2019
)
and its concurrent counterpart
(
Fesefeldt
et al. 2022
)
use weakest pre-expectation
(
Morgan
et al. 1996
)
style predicate transformers to derive expected values in probabilistic pointer programs. They rely on demonic nondeterminism for allocation—the expected value is lower bounded over all possible allocated addresses—and the frame rule gives a lower bound, whereas OSL is used for propositional reasoning, with each outcome having a likelihood.

Polaris
(
Tassarotti and
Harper 2019
)
incorporates probabilistic reasoning into Iris
(
Jung et al. 2015
)
and is also used to bound expected values using refinements inspired by probabilistic relational Hoare Logic
(
Barthe et al. 2015
)
. Polaris is limited to programs that terminate in finitely many steps and the program logic itself is only used to relate probabilistic programs to each other, whereas the quantitative reasoning about expected values must be done externally to the program logic.

Probabilistic Separation Logic (PSL)
(
Barthe
et al. 2019
)
and subsequent works
(
Bao
et al. 2021
;
Bao
et al. 2022
;
Li
et al. 2023
)
use an alternative model of separation to characterize probabilistic independence and related probability theoretic properties.
Doing so provides a compositional way to reason about probabilistic programs, though this work is orthogonal to our own as it does not deal with heaps.

Pulse and Incorrectness Separation Logic.

As recounted by
Raad et al. 2020
, Pulse uses under-approximation in four ways in order to achieve scalability:

(1)

Pulse takes advantage of the IL semantics in order to explore only one path at a time when the program execution branches, and to unroll loops for a bounded number of iterations.

(2)

Pulse elects to not consider cases in which memory is re-allocated.

(3)

Pulse uses under-approximate specifications for some library functions.

(4)

Pulse’s bi-abductive inference assumes that pointers are not aliased unless explicitly stated.

We have shown how (1) is achieved using OSL in the single path algorithm, (2) and (4) are standard assumptions in bi-abduction
(
Calcagno et al. 2009
;
Calcagno et al. 2011
)
(which we also use), and (3) is a corollary to (1), since the ability to drop paths opens the possibility for under-approximate procedure summaries.

Pulse does not support inductive predicates (
e.g.,
list segments), so it uses a simplified bi-abduction procedure capable of handling more types of pure assertions. This results in
exact
bi-abduction solutions; the inferred
M
and
F
satisfy
P\ast M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}\vDash Q\ast F
. As a result, Pulse does not use consequences to—since it is based on IL—strengthen the postcondition, meaning that the resulting specs can be interpreted as both ISL and OSL triples and that our algorithm from
Section

5
does accurately model Pulse. This finding was later confirmed by
Raad
et al. 2023
.

Unified Metatheory with Effects.

Our algebraic program semantics is similar to Weighted Programming
(
Batz et al. 2022
)
. Whereas we use an algebraic interpretation of choice to represent multiple types of (executable) program semantics, the goal of weighted programming is to specify mathematical models and find solutions to optimization problems via static analysis.

Cîrstea 2013
;
Cîrstea 2014
also used partial semirings to represent properties of program branching in coalgebraic logics.

Delaware et al. 2013a
;
Delaware et al. 2013b
developed 3MT, a unified framework in the Coq proof assistant for mechanizing metatheoretic proofs—such as type soundness–about languages with monadic effects. Our motivations are similar, but with the goal of developing program logics.

Unifying correctness and incorrectness.

In the time since
O’Hearn 2020
introduced Incorrectness Logic, a considerable amount of research attention has turned to unifying the theories of correctness and incorrectness.
Exact Separation Logic is one such approach, which combines the semantics of standard separation logic and Incorrectness Separation Logic to support correctness and incorrectness within a single program logic
(
Maksimović et al. 2023
)
. It is used within the Gillian symbolic execution system
(
Fragoso Santos et al. 2020
)
. The idea behind Exact Separation Logic is to create an analysis that is as precise as possible, up until it reaches a point where it must make the choice to move into correctness or incorrectness mode. However, the exact nature means that it does not have the flexibility to,
e.g.,
reason in abstract domains.

Local Completeness Logic is a related attempt to combine abstract interpretation with true bug-finding
(
Bruni
et al. 2021
;
Bruni
et al. 2023
)
. It uses a standard over-approximate abstract domain to analyze programs, combined with Incorrectness Logic to ensure that the over-approximation has not gotten too coarse so as to include false alarms. However, if a bug depends on nondeterminism (such as the malloc example in
Section

2
), then the over-approximate abstraction will preclude the analysis from
dropping paths
to only explore the trace where the error occurs, an ability that was identified by
O’Hearn 2020
as crucial to large scale bug-finding.

Though the goals of Exact Separation Logic and Local Completeness Logic are similar to our own, we take a substantially different approach. Combining Hoare and Incorrectness Logic inevitably yields something that is
less than the sum of its parts
; the resulting logic inherits all the limitations of both, compromising the ability to
approximate
the program behavior by either abstracting the current state or dropping paths. Instead of combining two existing logics, Outcome Separation Logic represents fundamentally new ideas and supports all the reasoning principles needed for efficient correctness and incorrectness analysis.

Like Exact Separation Logic and Local Completeness Logic, Outcome Separation Logic guarantees reachability for true-bug finding, while also covering all of the possible end states for correctness verification. But, unlike the aforementioned logics, it also supports weakening via standard forward consequences, so abstraction of the current state is always possible.
Abstraction is crucial to the scalability of techniques such as
shape analysis

(
Rinetzky and
Sagiv 2001
;
Berdine et al. 2007
)
, in which the analyzer attempts to prove memory safety while only tracking the
shapes
of data structures on the heap, not the data or length (like the list segment predicate from
Section

5
). Outcome Separation Logic makes shape analysis compatible with true bug finding for the first time. More generally, a key benefit of Outcome Separation Logic (over Exact Separation Logic and Local Completeness Logic) is that it can identify true bugs while retaining much less information, potentially leading to better scalability.

Hyper Hoare Logic
(
Dardinier and
Müller 2023
)
was designed to prove
hyperproperties
about programs by reasoning about multiple executions simultaneously. Examples of such properties include noninterference—guaranteeing that a program does not leak sensitive information. The design of Hyper Hoare Logic takes a similar approach to standard Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein
et al. 2023
;
Zilberstein 2024
)
, in which both correctness and incorrectness can be proven for a nondeterministic language. In fact, Hyper Hoare Logic has the same semantics as the nondeterministic instance of OL, though OL can also cover other effects such as probabilistic computation. Putting aside the semantic similarities between Outcome Logic and Hyper Hoare Logic, the goal of this particular paper was to augment OL with heaps, separation, local reasoning (via the frame rule), and bi-abduction—none of which are currently supported by Hyper Hoare Logic.

8.
Conclusion

Infer—based on separation logic and bi-abduction—is capable of efficiently analyzing industrial scale codebases, substantiating the idea that compositionality translates to real-world scalability
(
Calcagno et al. 2015
)
. But the deployment and integration of Infer into real-world engineering workflows also surfaced that proving the
absence
of bugs is somewhat of a red herring—software has bugs and sound logical theories are needed to find them
(
Le et al. 2022
)
.

Incorrectness Logic has shown that it is not only
possible
to formulate a theory for bug-finding, but it is in fact advantageous from a program analysis view; static analyzers can take certain liberties in searching for bugs that are not valid for correctness verification, such as dropping program paths for added efficiency. The downside is that the IL semantics is incompatible with correctness analysis, therefore separate implementations and procedure summaries must be used.

In OSL, we seek to get the best of both worlds.
As
Raad et al. 2020
put it, “aiming for under-approximate results rather than exact ones gives additional flexibility to the analysis designer, just as aiming for over-approximate rather than exact results does for correctness tools.”
The fact that OSL supports over-approximation in the traditional sense as well as under-approximation in the sense of Pulse
invites the reuse of tools between the two, while still enabling specialized techniques when needed (
i.e.,
loop invariants for correctness, dropping paths for incorrectness). In addition, OSL extends bi-abduction to programs with effects such as randomization for the first time.

OSL is not a simple extension of separation logic; it is designed from the ground up with new assumptions, since the properties that make the standard SL frame rule sound (nondeterministic allocation, must properties, and fault avoidance) are not suitable for reasoning about incorrectness and effects. The addition of tri-abduction to our symbolic execution algorithms also means that we can analyze more programs with control flow branching compared to Abductor.
The power and flexibility of OSL makes it a strong foundation for analyzing pointer-programs with effects.

Appendix

Appendix A
Program Semantics

We begin by providing definitions for natural orders, completeness and Scott continuity, which are mentioned in
Definition

3.3
.

Definition A.1 (Natural Ordering).

The
natural order
of a semiring
\langle A,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
is defined to be
a\leq b
iff
\exists a^{\prime}\in A.a+a^{\prime}=b
. A semiring is
naturally ordered
if
\leq
is a partial order.
Note that
\leq
is reflexive and transitive by the semiring laws, so it remains only to show that it is anti-symmetric. That is, if
a\leq b
and
b\leq a
, then
a=b
.

Definition A.2 (Complete Partial Semiring).

A partial semiring
\langle A,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
is complete if there is a sum operator
\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}
such that the following properties hold:

(1)

If
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}
is finite, then
\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}=x_{i_{1}}+\cdots+x_{i_{n}}

(2)

If
\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}
is defined, then
b\cdot\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}=\sum_{i\in I}b\cdot x_{i}
and
(\sum_{i\in I}x_{i})\cdot b=\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\cdot b
for any
b\in A

(3)

Let
(J_{k})_{k\in K}
be any family of nonempty disjoint subsets of
I
, so
I=\bigcup_{k\in K}J_{k}
and
J_{k}\cap J_{\ell}=\emptyset
if
k\neq\ell
. Then,
\sum_{k\in K}\sum_{j\in J_{k}}x_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}
.

This definition is adapted from
Golan 2003
.

Definition A.3 (Scott Continuity).

Consider a semiring
\langle A,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
with partial order
\leq
.
A function (or partial function)
f\colon A\to A
is Scott continuous if for any directed set
D\subseteq A
(where all pairs of elements in
D
have a supremum),
\sup_{a\in D}f(a)=f(\sup D)
.
A semiring is Scott continuous if
\sum
and
\cdot
are Scott continuous in both arguments
(
Karner 2004
)
.

In addition, we recall the definition of normalizable, which is stated as property (3) in
Definition

3.3
.

Definition A.4 (Normalizable).

A semiring
\langle A,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
is normalizable if for any well defined sum
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}
, there exists
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}=\dsser{1}
and
a_{i}=(\sum_{i\in I}a_{i})\cdot b_{i}
for every
i\in I
.

Normalizability is needed to show that relations lifted by the
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}
functor have several properties, for example that lifting is well behaved with respect to sums and scalar multiplication (
Lemmas

B.4
and
B.5
). We also show that normalization implies the weaker row-column property below:

Definition A.5 (Row-Column Property).

A monoid
\langle A,+,\dsser{0}\rangle
has the row-column property if for any two sequences of elements
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(b_{j})_{j\in J}
, if
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}=\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}
, then there exist
(u_{k})_{k\in I\times J}
such that
\sum_{j\in J}u_{(i,j)}=a_{i}
for all
i\in I
and
\sum_{i\in I}u_{(i,j)}=b_{j}
for all
j\in J
.

Lemma A.6.

Let
\langle A,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
be a normalizable semiring (
Definition

A.4
), then
\langle A,+,\dsser{0}\rangle
has the row-column property (
Definition

A.5
).

Proof.

Take any sequences
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(b_{j})_{j\in J}
such that
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}=\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}
and let
x=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}=\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}
. Now, since the semiring is normalizable, there must be
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(b^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in J}
such that
\sum_{i\in I}a^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j\in J}b^{\prime}_{j}=\dsser{1}
and
a_{i}=x\cdot a^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
and
b_{j}=x\cdot b^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\in J
. Let
u_{(i,j)}=x\cdot a^{\prime}_{i}\cdot b^{\prime}_{j}
. Now we have:

\sum_{i\in I}u_{(i,j)}=\sum_{i\in I}x\cdot a^{\prime}_{i}\cdot b^{\prime}_{j}=x\cdot(\sum_{i\in I}a^{\prime}_{i})\cdot b^{\prime}_{j}=x\cdot\dsser{1}\cdot b^{\prime}_{j}=x\cdot b^{\prime}_{j}=b_{j}

And

\sum_{j\in J}u_{(i,j)}=\sum_{j\in J}x\cdot a^{\prime}_{i}\cdot b^{\prime}_{j}=x\cdot a^{\prime}_{i}\cdot(\sum_{j\in J}b^{\prime}_{j})=x\cdot a^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\dsser{1}=x\cdot a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i}

∎

In fact, it is known that if some monoid
A
has the row-column property, then the functor
\mathcal{F}_{A}
of finitely supported maps into
A
preserves weak pullbacks
(
Gumm 2009
;
Moss 1999
;
Klin 2009
)
. It has also been shown that relations lifted by some functor preserve composition iff the functor preserves weak pullbacks
(
Kurz and Velebil 2016
)
. Combining these two results, we get that relations lifted by
\mathcal{F}_{A}
preserve composition if
A
has the row-column property.

In our case, the maps have countable support rather than finite, so the aforementioned results do not immediately apply, however they do provide evidence that the row-column property is a reasonable requirement. However, we require the stronger normalization property since lifted relations also must also be well behaved with respect to scalar multiplication (
Lemma

B.5
).

Remark 3
.

In
Definition

3.3
we require that
\sup(A)=\dsser{1}
, which limits the models to be contractive maps (the weight of the computation can only decrease as the program executes). This rules out, for example, real-valued multisets where the weights are elements of the semiring
\langle\mathbb{R}^{\infty},+,\cdot,0,1\rangle
. Still, there are more models than the ones we have presented including the tropical semiring
\langle\mathbb{R}_{0}^{\infty},\min,+,\infty,0\rangle
, which can be used to encode optimization problems
(
Batz et al. 2022
)
.

The fact that
\sup(A)=\dsser{1}
is used in order to define
\mathsf{Rep}
as a lifted relation (
Section

C.2
), and it is also used in the proof of
Lemma

C.5
.
It may be possible to relax this constraint, however the more general version is not needed for the models we explore in this paper.

A.1.
Proofs

Lemma A.7 (Scaled Sums).

If
\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}
is defined, then
\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\cdot y_{i}
is defined for any
(y_{i})_{i\in I}
.

Proof.

Since
\sup(A)
exists, then
y_{i}\leq\sup(A)
for each
i\in I
. By the definition of
\leq
, there exist
(y^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
y_{i}+y^{\prime}_{i}=\sup(A)
. We also know that
(\sum_{i\in I}x_{i})\cdot\sup(A)
exists, since
A
is closed under multiplication. Now, we have:

\displaystyle(\sum_{i\in I}x_{i})\cdot\sup(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\cdot(y_{i}+y^{\prime}_{i})

And by the semiring laws:

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\cdot y_{i}+x_{i}\cdot y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\cdot y_{i}+\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\cdot y^{\prime}_{i}

So, clearly the subexpression
{\sum_{i\in I}}x_{i}\cdot y_{i}
is defined.
∎

Lemma A.8 (Totality of Bind).

The
\mathsf{bind}
function defined in
Definition

3.7
is a total function (this is not immediate, since it uses partial addition).

Proof.

First, we note that
\sum_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(a)
must be defined by the definition of
\mathcal{W}
. By
Lemma

A.7
, we know that
\sum_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(a)\cdot f(a)(b)
must be defined too, for any family
(f(a)(b))_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}
. Now, since
\mathsf{bind}(m,f)(b)=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(a)\cdot f(a)(b)
, then it must be a total function. The result is also countably supported, since
m
and each
f(a)
are countably supported and
\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m,f))=\cup_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}\mathsf{supp}(f(a))
. Finally
|\mathsf{bind}(m,f)|
exists since:

\displaystyle|\mathsf{bind}(m,f)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m,f))}\sum_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(a)\cdot f(a)(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(a)\cdot\quad\sum_{b\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m,f))}f(a)(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(a)\cdot\sum_{b\in\mathsf{supp}(f(a))}f(a)(b)=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(a)\cdot|f(a)|

And the sum on the last line exists by
Lemma

A.7
.

∎

Theorem A.9 (Fixed Point Existence).

The function
F_{\langle C,e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}
defined in
Figure

1
has a least fixed point.

Proof.

It will suffice to show that
F_{\langle C,e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}
is Scott continuous, at which point, we can apply the Kleene fixed point theorem to conclude that the least fixed point exists. First, we define the pointwise order for
f_{1},f_{2}\colon\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{W}(\mathsf{St})
as
f_{1}\sqsubseteq f_{2}
iff
f_{1}(s,h)\sqsubseteq f_{2}(s,h)
for all
(s,h)
, where
f_{1}(s,h)\sqsubseteq f_{2}(s,h)
iff there exists
m
such that
f_{1}(s,h)+m=f_{2}(s,h)
.
Now, we will show that the monad bind is Scott continuous with respect to that order. Let
D
be a directed set.

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in D}\mathsf{bind}(m,f)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in D}\sum_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(s)\cdot\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f(a)&\text{if}~s=\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(a)\\
\mathsf{unit}_{M}(s)&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Now, by continuity of the semiring, suprema distribute over sums and products. It is relatively easy to see by induction that the supremum can move into every summand in the series.

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(s)\cdot\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sup_{f\in D}f(a)&\text{if}~s=\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(a)\\
\mathsf{unit}_{M}(s)&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(s)\cdot\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\sup D)(a)&\text{if}~s=\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(a)\\
\mathsf{unit}_{M}(s)&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\mathsf{bind}(m,\sup D)

Finally, we show that
F_{\langle C,e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}
is Scott continuous with respect to the order defined above.

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in D}F_{\langle C,e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}(f)

\displaystyle=\lambda(s,h).\sup_{f\in D}F_{\langle C,e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}(f)(s,h)

\displaystyle=\lambda(s,h).\sup_{f\in D}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h),f)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{1}\\
\mathsf{unit}(s,h)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{0}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\lambda(s,h).\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sup_{f\in D}\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h),f)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{1}\\
\sup_{f\in D}\mathsf{unit}(s,h)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{0}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\lambda(s,h).\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h),\sup D)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{1}\\
\mathsf{unit}(s,h)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{0}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=F_{\langle C,e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}(\sup D)

∎

Before proving that the semantics is total, we define the notion of compatible expressions.

Definition A.10 (Compatible Expressions).

Given some outcome algebra
\langle A,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
, two expressions
e
and
e^{\prime}
are
compatible
if
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(s)
is defined for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

Though compatibility is a semantic notion, there are straightforward syntactic checks to ensure that two expressions are compatible. For example, if
e
is a test—a Boolean-valued expression where
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)\in\{\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\}
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
—then
e
and
\lnot e
are compatible. In the probabilistic semiring,
p
and
1-p
are also compatible for any
p\in[0,1]
.

Theorem A.11 (Totality of Program Semantics).

Given an outcome algebra
\langle A,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
, the semantics of a program
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h)
is defined as long as the following conditions are met:

(1)

For each loop
\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C^{\prime}
appearing in
C
, the guard
e
must be a test. More precisely,
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)\in\{\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\}
for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

(2)

If the semiring addition is partial, then each use of
+
in
C
must be guarded by compatible expressions. That is, they must have the form
(\mathsf{assume}~e_{1}\fatsemi C_{1})+(\mathsf{assume}~e_{2}\fatsemi C_{2})
where
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are compatible according to
Definition

A.10
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
C
. The cases for
\mathsf{skip}
,
\mathsf{assume}
, and all atoms are trivial.

\triangleright

C=C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}
. By the induction hypothesis, we assume
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}
are total, and by
Lemma

A.8
we know that bind is total, therefore
\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h),\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}})
is total.

\triangleright

C=C_{1}+C_{2}
. If the semiring addition is total, then this case follows trivially from the induction hypothesis. If not, then the program must have the form
(\mathsf{assume}~e_{1}\fatsemi C_{1})+(\mathsf{assume}~e_{2}\fatsemi C_{2})
where
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are compatible, so we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket(\mathsf{assume}~e_{1}\fatsemi C_{1})+(\mathsf{assume}~e_{2}\fatsemi C_{2})\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s)\cdot\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket(s,h)+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s)\cdot\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket(s,h)

By the induction hypothesis,
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket(s,h)
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket(s,h)
are defined. Since
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are compatible,
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s)+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s)
is defined too. So, the claim follows by
Lemma

A.7
.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C
. Follows from
Theorem

A.9
.

∎

Appendix B
Properties of Lifted Relations

Lemma B.1.

If
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{R}
, then
|m_{1}|=|m_{2}|
.

Proof.

We know that there exists an
m
such that:

m_{1}=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,y)\qquad\text{and}\qquad m_{2}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(x,y)

Now, we have:

\displaystyle|m_{1}|

\displaystyle=|\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,y)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(x,y)

\displaystyle=|\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(x,y)|=|m_{2}|

∎

Lemma B.2.

(\dsser{0},m)\in\overline{R}
iff
m=\dsser{0}
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)

We know there must be some
m^{\prime}
such that
m=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\dsser{0})}m^{\prime}(x,y)
, but since
\mathsf{supp}(\dsser{0})=\emptyset
, then
m=\dsser{0}
.

(\Leftarrow)

Let
m^{\prime}=\dsser{0}
, so clearly
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}R
and we also have
\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(\dsser{0})}(\dsser{0}(x,y))=\dsser{0}
and
\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\dsser{0})}(\dsser{0}(x,y))=\dsser{0}

∎

Lemma B.3.

If
|m_{1}|+|m_{2}|
is defined, then
m_{1}+m_{2}
is defined.

Proof.

Observe that:

\displaystyle|m_{1}|+|m_{2}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m_{1}(x)+\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m_{2}(x)

By associativity:

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})\cup\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m_{1}(x)+m_{2}(x)

Therefore
m_{1}(x)+m_{2}(x)
is defined for all
x
, and
|m_{1}+m_{2}|
is defined as well, so
m_{1}+m_{2}
is defined.
∎

Lemma B.4.

(m_{1}+m_{2},m^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
iff there exist
m^{\prime}_{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}
and
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{R}
and
(m_{2},m^{\prime}_{2})\in\overline{R}
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)

We know that there is some
m
such that
m_{1}+m_{2}=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}m(x,y)
and
m^{\prime}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}+m_{2})}m(x,y)
.
So, for each
x
, we have that
m_{1}(x)+m_{2}(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}m(x,y)
. Using
Lemma

A.6
, we know there must be
(x_{k})_{k\in\{1,2\}\times\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}
such that
m_{i}(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}x_{(i,y)}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
m(x,y)=x_{(1,y)}+x_{(2,y)}
for each
y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
.
Now let
m_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=x_{(1,y)}
and
m^{\prime\prime}_{2}(x,y)=x_{(2,y)}
and
let
m_{1}^{\prime}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)
and
m_{2}^{\prime}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)
. First, we establish that
m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}=m^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)+\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}x_{(1,y)}+\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}x_{(2,y)}

If
x\notin\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})
, then
x_{(1,y)}=\dsser{0}
and similarly for
x_{(2,y)}
and
\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})
, so we can combine the sums

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}+m_{2})}x_{(1,y)}+x_{(2,y)}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}+m_{2})}m(x,y)=m^{\prime}

Now, observe that:

\displaystyle\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{i})}m_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{i})}x_{(i,y)}

Now, for any
y\notin\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{i})
, it must be the case that
m^{\prime}_{i}(y)=\dsser{0}
and so
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{i})}x_{(i,y)}=\dsser{0}
, so each
x_{(i,y)}=\dsser{0}
. This means we can expand the sum to be over
\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2})=\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}x_{(i,y)}=m_{i}

We also know that
m_{i}^{\prime}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{i})}m_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)
by definition, so
(m_{i},m^{\prime}_{i})\in\overline{R}
.

(\Leftarrow)

We know that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{R}
and
(m_{2},m^{\prime}_{2})\in\overline{R}
, so there are
m^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
such that
m_{i}=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{i})}m^{\prime\prime}_{i}(x,y)
and
m^{\prime}_{i}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{i})}m^{\prime\prime}_{i}(x,y)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
m^{\prime\prime}=m^{\prime\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
(we can conclude that this is defined using
Lemmas

B.1
and
B.3
). Now we have the following:

\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)+m_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{1})}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)+\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=m_{1}+m_{2}

\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}+m_{2})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}+m_{2})}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)+m_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)+\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}=m^{\prime}

So,
(m_{1}+m_{2},m^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
.

∎

Lemma B.5.

(a\cdot m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{R}
iff there exists
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
m_{2}=a\cdot m^{\prime}_{2}
and
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{2})\in\overline{R}

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)

By the definition of relation lifting, there is an
m
such that
a\cdot m_{1}=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,y)
and
m_{2}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(a\cdot m_{1})}m(x,y)
. This means that for all
x
:

a\cdot m_{1}(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,y)

By
Definition

A.4
, we can obtain
(b_{(x,y)})_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}
such that
\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}b_{(x,y)}=\dsser{1}
and
m(x,y)=({\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}}m(x,z))\cdot b_{(x,y)}
for all
y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})
. Now, define
m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=m_{1}(x)\cdot b_{(x,y)}
and
m_{2}^{\prime}(y)=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)
.
We now show that
m_{2}=a\cdot m^{\prime}_{2}
:

\displaystyle a\cdot m^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda y.a\cdot\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\lambda y.a\cdot\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m_{1}(x)\cdot b_{(x,y)}

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}a\cdot m_{1}(x)\cdot b_{(x,y)}

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}(\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,z))\cdot b_{(x,y)}

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(x,y)=m_{2}

We also have:

\displaystyle\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})}m_{1}(x)\cdot b_{(x,y)}

Since we already showed that
m_{2}=a\cdot m^{\prime}_{2}
, then it must be the case that
\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})=\mathsf{supp}(m_{2}^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle=\lambda x.m_{1}(x)\cdot\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}b_{(x,y)}=\lambda x.m_{1}(x)\cdot\dsser{1}=m_{1}

And clearly
m_{2}^{\prime}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)
by definition, so
(m_{1},m_{2}^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
.

(\Leftarrow)

By the definition of relation lifting, there is some
m
such that
m_{1}=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})}m(x,y)
and
m_{2}^{\prime}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(x,y)
.
Now, let
m^{\prime}=a\cdot m
, so this clearly means that
a\cdot m_{1}=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(a\cdot m^{\prime}_{2})}m^{\prime}(x,y)
and
a\cdot m^{\prime}_{2}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(a\cdot m_{1})}m^{\prime}(x,y)
, so
(a\cdot m_{1},a\cdot m^{\prime}_{2})\in\overline{R}
.

∎

Lemma B.6.

If
(x,y)\in R
, then
(\mathsf{unit}(x),\mathsf{unit}(y))\in\overline{R}

Proof.

Let
m=\mathsf{unit}(x,y)
. We therefore have:

\lambda x^{\prime}.\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{unit}(y))}m(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\lambda x^{\prime}.\mathsf{unit}(x,y)(x^{\prime},y)=\mathsf{unit}(x)

And similarly,
\lambda y^{\prime}.\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{unit}(x))}m(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\mathsf{unit}(y)
, so
(\mathsf{unit}(x),\mathsf{unit}(y))\in\overline{R}
.
∎

Lemma B.7.

For any relations
R\subseteq X\times Y
and
S\subseteq Y\times Z
, if
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{S\circ R}
, then
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{S}\circ\overline{R}
.

Proof.

Suppose
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{S\circ R}
, so there is some
m\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}(S\circ R)
such that
m_{1}=\lambda x.\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,z)
and
m_{2}=\lambda z.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(x,z)
. This means that for each
(x,z)\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
, there is some
y
such that
(x,y)\in R
and
(y,z)\in S
. Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
be some relation where we choose one
y
for each
z
, so that
|\{y\mid(y,z)\in S^{\prime}\}|=1
for each
z
and
\{z\mid\exists y.(y,z)\in S^{\prime}\}=\{z\mid\exists y.(y,z)\in S\}
. Now, let:

m^{\prime}(y,z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}m_{2}(z)&\text{if}~(y,z)\in S^{\prime}\\
\dsser{0}&\text{if}~(y,z)\notin S^{\prime}\end{array}\right.\quad m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})\mid(y,z)\in S^{\prime}}m(x,z)\quad m_{3}=\lambda y.\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m^{\prime}(y,z)

And now, we have the following:

\displaystyle\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{3})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{3})}\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})\mid(y,z)\in S^{\prime}}m(x,z)

Since
S^{\prime}
relates each
z
to exactly one
y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{3})
, this is equivalent to summing over all
z

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(x,z)=m_{1}

\displaystyle\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m^{\prime\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})\mid(y,z)\in S^{\prime}}m(x,z)

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})\mid(y,z)\in S^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(x,z)

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})\mid(y,z)\in S^{\prime}}m_{2}(z)=\lambda y.\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m^{\prime}(y,z)=m_{3}

So,
(m_{1},m_{3})\in\overline{R}
. Also, by definition we know that
m_{3}=\lambda y.\sum_{z\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m^{\prime}(y,z)
and
m_{2}=\lambda z.{\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{3})}}m^{\prime}(y,z)
since
m^{\prime}(y,z)
is nonzero for exactly one
y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{3})
and is equal to
m_{2}(z)
at that point. This means that
(m_{3},m_{2})\in\overline{S}
, therefore
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{S}\circ\overline{R}
.

∎

Lemma B.8.

For any pair of directed chains
m_{(i,1)}\sqsubseteq m_{(i,2)}\sqsubseteq\cdots
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
if
(m_{(1,n)},m_{(2,n)})\in\overline{R}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(1,n)},\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(2,n)})\in\overline{R}
.

Proof.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we know that there is an
m_{n}
such that
m_{(1,n)}=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{(2,n)})}m_{n}(x,y)
and
m_{(2,n)}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{(1,n)})}m_{n}(x,y)
.
Since each
(m_{(i,n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a chain,
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(i,n)}
exists, and:

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(1,n)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{(2,n)})}m_{n}(x,y))

Since we use a pointwise order for functions, the
\sup
of a function is equal to the
\sup
at each point. Additionally, since the semiring is continuous, the
\sup
distributes over the sum.

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(2,n)})}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}(x,y)

And by a similar argument,
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(2,n)}=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(1,n)})}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}(x,y)
, so:

(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(1,n)},\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{(2,n)})\in\overline{R}

∎

Appendix C
Outcome Separation Logic

Lemma C.1 (Normalization).

For any
m\neq\dsser{0}
, there exists
m^{\prime}
such that
|m^{\prime}|=\dsser{1}
and
m=|m|\cdot m^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By property (3) of
Definition

3.3
, there must be
(b_{s})_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}
such that
m(s)=(\sum_{t\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(t))\cdot b_{s}
and
\sum_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}b_{s}=\dsser{1}
. Now, let
m^{\prime}
be defined as follows:

m^{\prime}(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}b_{s}&\text{if}~s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)\\
\dsser{0}&\text{if}~s\notin\mathsf{supp}(m)\end{array}\right.

So, clearly
|m^{\prime}|=\sum_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}b_{s}=\dsser{1}
. For every
s
, we also have
m(s)=(\sum_{t\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(t))\cdot b_{s}=|m|\cdot b_{s}=|m|\cdot m^{\prime}(s)
, so
m=|m|\cdot m^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma C.2 (Splitting).

If
|m^{\prime}|\leq|m_{1}|+|m_{2}|
, then there exist
m^{\prime}_{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
|m^{\prime}_{1}|\leq|m_{1}|
and
|m_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|m_{2}|
and
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
|m_{1}|+|m_{2}|\geq|m^{\prime}|
, then there is some
a
such that:

|m_{1}|+|m_{2}|=|m^{\prime}|+a=a+\sum_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}m^{\prime}(s)

So, by
Lemma

A.6
, there exists
(u_{k})_{k\in\{1,2\}\times(1+\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}))}
such that for all
i\in\{1,2\}
and
s\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
:

|m_{i}|=\sum_{s\in 1+\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}u_{(i,s)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad a=u_{(1,\star)}+u_{(2,\star)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad m^{\prime}(s)=u_{(1,s)}+u_{(2,s)}

Now, let
m^{\prime}_{i}=\lambda s.u_{(i,s)}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. So,
m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}=\lambda s.u_{(1,s)}+u_{(2,s)}=m^{\prime}
. Now, it just remains to show that
|m_{i}|\geq|m^{\prime}_{i}|
:

|m_{i}|=\sum_{s\in 1+\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}u_{(i,s)}=u_{(i,\star)}+\sum_{s\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{i}(s)=u_{(i,\star)}+|m^{\prime}_{i}|\geq|m^{\prime}_{i}|

∎

C.1.
The Outcome Separating Conjunction

See
4.3

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
. Since
\varphi\circledast F=\top
, then clearly
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi\circledast F

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
. We know
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
or
m\vDash\varphi_{2}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}\circledast F
. We can therefore weaken this to conclude that
m^{\prime}\vDash(\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2})\circledast F
. The case where
m\vDash\varphi_{2}
is symmetrical.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}
. We know that
m_{1}\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
m_{2}\vDash\varphi_{2}
for some
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
.
Now, since
(m_{1}+m_{2},m^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
, by

B.4
there must be
m^{\prime}_{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
(m_{2},m^{\prime}_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}_{1}\vDash\varphi_{1}\circledast F
and
m^{\prime}_{2}\vDash\varphi_{2}\circledast F
, so
m^{\prime}\vDash(\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2})\circledast F
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi^{\prime(a)}
. We know that
a=\dsser{0}
and
m=\dsser{0}
or
m_{1}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
for some
m_{1}
such that
m=a\cdot m_{1}
.
In the first case, by
Lemma

B.2
,
m^{\prime}=\dsser{0}
and so clearly
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi^{\prime(\dsser{0})}\circledast F
.
In the second case, since
(a\cdot m_{1},m^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(A)}
, by
Lemma

B.5
there must be
m^{\prime}_{1}
such that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
m^{\prime}=a\cdot m^{\prime}_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}_{1}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}\circledast F
, so therefore
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi^{\prime(a)}\circledast F
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\epsilon:P
. We know that
|m|=\dsser{1}
and every
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
has the form
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h)
such that
(s,h)\in P
.
Since
(m,m^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
, we know by
Lemma

B.1
that
|m^{\prime}|=|m|=\dsser{1}
.
Additionally, for every element in
\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
, there must be an element in
\mathsf{supp}(m)
related by
\mathsf{frame}(F)
, so each element of
m^{\prime}
has the form
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime})
such that
(s,h)\in P
and
(s,h^{\prime})\in F
, and so clearly
(s,h\uplus h^{\prime})\in P\ast F
, and therefore also
m^{\prime}\vDash(\epsilon:P)\circledast F
.

∎

See
4.4

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
. Suppose
m\vDash\top\circledast F
. Let
m_{1}=m^{\prime}_{1}=\dsser{0}
and
m_{2}=m
. Clearly
(\dsser{0},\dsser{0})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
m^{\prime}_{1}+m_{2}=\dsser{0}+m=m
. Now, taking any
m_{2}^{\prime}
, it is obvious that
\dsser{0}+m_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\top
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
. We know
m\vDash\varphi_{1}\circledast F
or
m\vDash\varphi_{2}\circledast F
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis, there are
m_{1}
,
m^{\prime}_{1}
, and
m_{2}
such that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
m=m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}
and
m_{1}+m_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}
for any
m_{2}^{\prime}
such that
|m_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|m_{2}|
.
Now, take any such
m_{2}^{\prime}
, we know that
m_{1}+m_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}
. We can weaken this to conclude that
m_{1}+m_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}
.
We know that there are
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\vDash\varphi_{1}\circledast F
and
m_{2}\vDash\varphi_{2}\circledast F
and
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
.
By the induction hypotheses, we get that there are
u_{1},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2},v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime}
, and
v_{2}
such that
m_{1}=u_{1}^{\prime}+u_{2}
and
m_{2}^{\prime}=v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{2}
and
(u_{1},u_{1}^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
(v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
u_{1}+u_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
v_{1}+v_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{2}
whenever
|u_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|u_{2}|
and
|v_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|v_{2}|
.

Now, let
m_{1}=u_{1}+v_{1}
and
m_{1}^{\prime}=u_{1}^{\prime}+v_{1}^{\prime}
and
m_{2}=u_{2}+v_{2}
. By
Lemma

B.4
, we get that
(m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
. We also have that:

m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}=(u_{1}^{\prime}+v_{1}^{\prime})+(u_{2}+v_{2})=(u_{1}^{\prime}+u_{2})+(v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{2})=m_{1}+m_{2}=m

Now, take any
m_{2}^{\prime}
such that
|m_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|m_{2}|=|u_{2}|+|v_{2}|
. By
Lemma

C.2
we know that there are
u_{2}^{\prime}
and
v_{2}^{\prime}
such that
|u_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|u_{2}|
and
|v_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|v_{2}|
and
m_{2}^{\prime}=u_{2}^{\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime}
. This means that
u_{1}+u_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
v_{1}+v_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{2}
. We also have:

(u_{1}+u_{2}^{\prime})+(v_{1}+v_{2}^{\prime})=(u_{1}+v_{1})+(u_{2}^{\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime})=m_{1}+m_{2}^{\prime}

So,
m_{1}+m_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi^{\prime(a)}
. We know that there is
m^{\prime}
such that
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
and
m=a\cdot m^{\prime}
. By the induction hypothesis, we get that there are
u_{1}
,
u^{\prime}_{1}
, and
u_{2}

(u_{1},u^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
,

whenever
|u_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|u_{2}|
.

Now, let

,

m^{\prime}_{1}+m_{2}=a\cdot u_{1}^{\prime}+a\cdot u_{2}=a\cdot(u^{\prime}_{1}+u_{2})=a\cdot m^{\prime}=m

Now, take any
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
|m^{\prime}_{2}|\leq|m_{2}|=a\cdot|u_{2}|
.
If
m^{\prime}_{2}=\dsser{0}
, then
m_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}=m_{1}=a\cdot u_{1}
, so

. If not, then by
Lemma

C.1
there is
m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
such that
|m^{\prime\prime}_{2}|=\dsser{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}=|m^{\prime}_{2}|\cdot m_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.
By the definition of
\leq
, there must be
a^{\prime}
such that
|m^{\prime}_{2}|+a^{\prime}=a\cdot|u_{2}|
and by
Definition

A.4
, there must be a

such that
|m^{\prime}_{2}|=(|m^{\prime}_{2}|+a^{\prime})\cdot b=a\cdot|u_{2}|\cdot b
.
Now, let
u^{\prime}_{2}=|u_{2}|\cdot b\cdot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, so
|u^{\prime}_{2}|=|u_{2}|\cdot b\cdot\dsser{1}\leq|u_{2}|
, and therefore
u_{1}+u^{\prime}_{2}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
and also

. Finally, we have that:

a\cdot(u_{1}+u^{\prime}_{2})=a\cdot u_{1}+a\cdot|u_{2}|\cdot b\cdot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}=m_{1}+|m^{\prime}_{2}|\cdot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}=m_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}

So, we get that that
m_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}\vDash\varphi^{\prime(a)}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\epsilon:P
. We know that
m\vDash\epsilon:P\ast F
, so
|m|=\dsser{1}
and every element of
\mathsf{supp}(m)
has the form
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime})
such that
(s,h)\in P
and
(s,h^{\prime})\in F
. Now, let
m_{2}=\dsser{0}
,
m^{\prime}_{1}=m
, so clearly
m=m^{\prime}_{1}+m_{2}
.
To define
m_{1}
, first we fix a relation
S\subseteq\mathsf{frame}(F)
such that for any
h^{\prime\prime}
such that
(s,h^{\prime\prime})\in P\ast F
, there is a unique
h
and
h^{\prime}
such that
h^{\prime\prime}=h\uplus h^{\prime}
and
(s,h)\in P
and
(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h),\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime}))\in S
. Now,
m_{1}
is defined as follows:

m_{1}(\sigma)=\sum_{\tau\mid(\sigma,\tau)\in S}m(\tau)

Now, we must show that
(m_{1},m)\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
. To do so, first let:

m^{\prime}(\sigma,\tau)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}m(\tau)&\text{if}~(\sigma,\tau)\in S\\
\dsser{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Now, we have:

\displaystyle\lambda\sigma.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m^{\prime}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=\lambda\sigma.\sum_{\tau\mid(\sigma,\tau)\in S}m(\tau)=m_{1}

\displaystyle\lambda\tau.\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m^{\prime}(\sigma,\tau)
