\displaystyle=\lambda\tau.\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}m(\tau)&\text{if}~(\sigma,\tau)\in S\\
\dsser{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

By the definition of
S
, there is exactly one
\sigma
for each
\tau
such that
(\sigma,\tau)\in S
, so:

\displaystyle=\lambda\tau.m(\tau)=m

So,
(m_{1},m)\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
, and this also means that
|m_{1}|=|m|=\dsser{1}
. Also, by construction, each element of
\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})
has the form
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h)
where
(s,h)\in P
, so
m_{1}\vDash\epsilon:P
. Since
m_{2}=\dsser{0}
, then it follows that
m_{1}+m_{2}^{\prime}\vDash\epsilon:P
for any
m_{2}^{\prime}
such that
|m_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|m_{2}|=\dsser{0}
.

∎

Now, for the proof of the frame property, it will be necessary to relate states based not only on whether they can be obtained by augmenting the heap, but we must also ensure that after augmenting the heap, it is possible to observe the same allocation behavior. To that end, we introduce a slightly modified
\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}
relation.

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})=\\
\quad\left\{(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s[X\mapsto n],h),\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(s,h^{\prime\prime})\vDash F\\
\forall(s_{0},h_{0}).~s_{0}(x)=n\Rightarrow\\
\quad\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s_{0},h_{0})=\mathsf{alloc}(s_{0}[X\mapsto s(X)],h_{0}\uplus h^{\prime\prime})\end{array}\right\}\cup\\
\quad\{(\mathsf{undef},\mathsf{undef})\}\end{array}

Lemma C.3.

If
m\vDash\varphi
,
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi)
and
(m,m^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})}
, then
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi\circledast F

Proof.

Let
R=\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})
. The proof is by induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
. Since
\varphi\circledast F=\top
, then clearly
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi\circledast F

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
. We know
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
or
m\vDash\varphi_{2}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}\circledast F
. We can therefore weaken this to conclude that
m^{\prime}\vDash(\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2})\circledast F
. The case where
m\vDash\varphi_{2}
is symmetrical.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}
. We know that
m_{1}\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
m_{2}\vDash\varphi_{2}
for some
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
.
Now, since
(m_{1}+m_{2},m^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
, by
Lemma

B.4
there must be
m^{\prime}_{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{R}
and
(m_{2},m^{\prime}_{2})\in\overline{R}
and
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}_{1}\vDash\varphi_{1}\circledast F
and
m^{\prime}_{2}\vDash\varphi_{2}\circledast F
, so
m^{\prime}\vDash(\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2})\circledast F
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi^{\prime(a)}
. If
a=\dsser{0}
, then
m=\dsser{0}
and by
Lemma

B.2

m^{\prime}=\dsser{0}
, so
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi^{\prime(\dsser{0})}\circledast F
. Otherwise, we know that
m_{1}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
for some
m_{1}
such that
m=a\cdot m_{1}
. Since
(a\cdot m_{1},m^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
, by
Lemma

B.5
there must be an
m^{\prime}_{1}
such that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{R}
and
m^{\prime}=a\cdot m^{\prime}_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}_{1}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}\circledast F
, so
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi^{\prime(a)}\circledast F
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\epsilon:P
. We know that
|m|=\dsser{1}
and every
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
has the form
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s[X\mapsto n],h)
such that
(s[X\mapsto n],h)\in P
, and since
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(P)
, then it must also be that
(s,h)\in P
.
Since
(m,m^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
, we know by
Lemma

B.1
that
|m^{\prime}|=|m|=\dsser{1}
.
Additionally, for every element in
\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
, there must be an element in
\mathsf{supp}(m)
related by
R
, so each element of
m^{\prime}
has the form
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime})
such that
(s,h^{\prime})\in F
, and so clearly
(s,h\uplus h^{\prime})\in P\ast F
, and therefore also
m^{\prime}\vDash(\epsilon:P)\circledast F
.

∎

Lemma C.4.

For any
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi)
, and
\mathsf{alloc}\in\mathsf{Alloc}
, if
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
and
m_{1}+u\vDash\varphi
, then there exists an
m^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}
such that
(m^{\prime}_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})}
and
m^{\prime}_{1}+u\vDash\varphi
.

Proof.

Since
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}(F)}
, there is some
m
such that:

m_{1}=\lambda\sigma.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(\sigma,\tau)\quad\text{and}\quad m_{2}=\lambda\tau.\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)

Since
m
must by countably supported, we can enumerate the elements of
\mathsf{supp}(m)
, assigning each a unique natural number
n
. Let
f\colon\mathsf{supp}(m)\to\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid 1\leq n\leq|\mathsf{supp}(m)|\}
be such a bijection, and note that
\mathbb{N}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathsf{Val}
, so the codomain of
f
contains valid program values. Now, let
m^{\prime}
be defined as follows:

\displaystyle m^{\prime}(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h),\tau)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}m(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h),\tau)&\text{if}~\exists s^{\prime}.f^{-1}(s(X))=(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h),\tau)\land s=s^{\prime}[X\mapsto s(X)]\\
\dsser{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle m^{\prime}(\mathsf{undef},\tau)

\displaystyle=m(\mathsf{undef},\tau)

Intuitively
m^{\prime}
is obtained by taking each
(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h),\tau)
in the support of
m
and updating
s
so that
X
has value
f(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h),\tau)
. Now, we define
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}
as follows:

\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s,h)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{alloc}(s[X\mapsto s^{\prime}(x)],h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})&\text{if}~\exists s^{\prime},h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}.~f^{-1}(s(X))=(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\\
\mathsf{alloc}(s,h)&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

First, we argue that
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Alloc}
. It is obvious that
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s,h)
cannot return anything in the domain of
h
, since its definition relies on
\mathsf{alloc}
, run on an even larger heap. In the definition of
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}
, there are two cases. In the first case,
|\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s,h)|=|\mathsf{alloc}(s[X\mapsto s^{\prime}(x)],h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})|=\dsser{1}
and in the second case,
|\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s,h)|=|\mathsf{alloc}(s,h)|=\dsser{1}
.

We now show that
\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})
. Take any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
. If
\sigma=\mathsf{undef}
, then it must be the case that
\tau=\mathsf{undef}
too, since
m^{\prime}(\mathsf{undef},\tau)=m(\mathsf{undef},\tau)
and
\mathsf{supp}(m)\subseteq\mathsf{frame}(F)
, which only relates
\mathsf{undef}
to itself, therefore
(\sigma,\tau)=(\mathsf{undef},\mathsf{undef})\in\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})
.

If instead
\sigma=\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h)
, then there must be some
s^{\prime}
such that
f^{-1}(s(X))=(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h),\tau)
and
s=s^{\prime}[X\mapsto s(X)]
. By the definition of
f
, that means that
(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h),\tau)\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
, and so
\tau=\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})
for some
h^{\prime\prime}
such that
(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})\in F
. In addition, by the definition of
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}
, for any
s_{0}
and
h_{0}
such that
s_{0}(X)=s(X)
,
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s_{0},h_{0})=\mathsf{alloc}(s_{0}[X\mapsto s^{\prime}(X)],h_{0}\uplus h^{\prime\prime})
, therefore:

(\sigma,\tau)=(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto s(X)],h),\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})

Now, let
m_{1}^{\prime}=\lambda\sigma.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m^{\prime}(\sigma,\tau)
and observe that for all
\tau
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}^{\prime})}m^{\prime}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=m^{\prime}(\mathsf{undef},\tau)+\sum_{\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h)\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}^{\prime})}m^{\prime}(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h),\tau)

\displaystyle=m(\mathsf{undef},\tau)+\sum_{\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h)\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1}^{\prime})}\sum_{v\in\mathsf{Val}}m(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s[X\mapsto v],h),\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)=m_{2}(\tau)

So, we conclude that
(m^{\prime}_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})}
. Finally, for any
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h)
:

\displaystyle m_{1}(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h),\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m^{\prime}(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s[X\mapsto f(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h))],h),\tau)

\displaystyle=m_{1}^{\prime}(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s[X\mapsto f(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s,h))],h))

So
m_{1}
and
m_{1}^{\prime}
differ only in the values of
X
, which does not appear in
\varphi
by assumption, so
m^{\prime}_{1}\vDash\varphi
.

∎

C.2.
Replacement of Unsafe States

First, we provide the definitions of the
\mathsf{Rep}
relation and the
\mathsf{prune}
operation.
\mathsf{Rep}
relates
\mathsf{undef}
to any other state and
\lightning
, indicating that after framing, an
\mathsf{undef}
state can become any other state, or can diverge (
\lightning
). All
\mathsf{ok}
and
\mathsf{er}
states are only related to themselves. The
\mathsf{prune}\colon\mathcal{W}(\mathsf{St}\cup\{\lightning\})\to\mathcal{W}(\mathsf{St})
function removes
\lightning
from some program configuration
m
.

\mathsf{Rep}=\{(\mathsf{undef},\sigma)\mid\sigma\in\mathsf{St}\cup\{\lightning\}\}\cup\{(\sigma,\sigma)\mid\sigma\in\mathsf{St}\}\qquad\quad\mathsf{prune}(m)(\sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}m(\sigma)&\text{if}~\sigma\neq\lightning\\
\dsser{0}&\text{if}~\sigma=\lightning\end{array}\right.

Now, by lifting the
\mathsf{Rep}
relation, we can prove that replacing undefined states in some program configuration does not affect the validity of outcome assertions. Intuitively, this is true because
\mathsf{undef}
can only be satisfied by
\top
, which is also satisfied by anything else.

See
4.5

Proof.

By induction on the assertion
\varphi
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
.
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})\vDash\varphi
since everything satisfies
\top
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
. Without loss of generality, suppose
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})\vDash\varphi_{1}
. We can weaken this to conclude that
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})\vDash\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}
. We know that
m_{1}\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
m_{2}\vDash\varphi_{2}
for some
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m_{1}+m_{2}=m
.
Since
(m_{1}+m_{2},m^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{Rep}}
, there must be some
m^{\prime}_{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{\mathsf{Rep}}
and
(m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{Rep}}
and
m^{\prime}=m_{1}^{\prime}+m^{\prime}_{2}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{1})\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{2})\vDash\varphi_{2}
. It is easy to see that
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{1})+\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{2})=\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2})=\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})
, therefore
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})\vDash\varphi_{1}\oplus_{a}\varphi_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi^{\prime(a)}
. If
a=\dsser{0}
, then
m=\dsser{0}
, and by
Lemma

B.2

m^{\prime}=\dsser{0}
too. So
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})=\dsser{0}
and
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})\vDash\varphi^{\prime(\dsser{0})}
.
Otherwise, we know that there is an
m_{1}
such that
m_{1}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
and
m=a\cdot m_{1}
. Since
(a\cdot m_{1},m^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{Rep}}
, by
Lemma

B.5
we know there must be
m^{\prime}_{1}
such that
m^{\prime}=a\cdot m^{\prime}_{1}
and
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{\mathsf{Rep}}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{1})\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
, so—since
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})=\mathsf{prune}(a\cdot m^{\prime}_{1})=a\cdot\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{1})
—we get that
\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})\vDash\varphi^{\prime(a)}\circledast F
.

\triangleright

\varphi=(\epsilon:P)
. By definition,
\mathsf{undef}\notin\mathsf{supp}(m)
, so
m^{\prime}=m
(since
\mathsf{Rep}
only relates defined states to themselves), which satisfies
\epsilon:P
by assumption.

∎

C.3.
The Frame Rule

Lemma C.5 (Sequencing).

For any
f,g\colon\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{St})
and relation
R\subseteq\mathsf{St}\times\mathsf{St}\cup\{\lightning\}
, if
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{R}
and:

\forall(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s_{1},h_{1}),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s_{2},h_{2}))\in R.\quad\exists m.\quad g(s_{2},h_{2})=\mathsf{prune}(m)\quad\text{and}\quad(f(s_{1},h_{1}),m)\in\overline{R}

Then, there exists
m_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{bind}(\mathsf{prune}(m_{2}),g)=\mathsf{prune}(m_{2}^{\prime})
and
(\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f),m_{2}^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
.

Proof.

First, let
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{St}\to\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{St})
be defined as follows:

f^{\prime}(\sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f(s,h)&\text{if}~\sigma=\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h)\\
\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\sigma)&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\qquad g^{\prime}(\sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g(s,h)&\text{if}~\sigma=\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h)\\
\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\sigma)&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Note that
\mathsf{bind}(m,f)=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathcal{W}}(m,f^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{bind}(m,g)=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathcal{W}}(m,g^{\prime})
for all
m
.
Now, we argue that if
(\sigma,\tau)\in R
, then there exists
m_{\sigma,\tau}
such that
g^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathsf{prune}(m_{\sigma,\tau})
and
(f^{\prime}(\sigma),m_{\sigma,\tau})\in\overline{R}
.
We do so by case analysis.

\triangleright

\sigma=\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h)
and
\tau=\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
. By definition,
g^{\prime}(\tau)=g(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
and
f^{\prime}(\sigma)=f(s,h)
, so the claim holds by assumption.

\triangleright

\sigma=\mathsf{undef}
. In this case,
f^{\prime}(\sigma)=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathsf{undef})
, which means that
f^{\prime}(\sigma)
is related to all configurations of size
\dsser{1}
according to
\overline{R}
. Now, since
\sup(A)=\dsser{1}
, it must be that
|g^{\prime}(\tau)|\leq\dsser{1}
and so there is some
u
such that
|g^{\prime}(\tau)|+u=\dsser{1}
. Now, let
m_{\sigma,\tau}=g^{\prime}(\tau)+u\cdot\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\lightning)
, so clearly
g^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathsf{prune}(m_{\sigma,\tau})
and
(f^{\prime}(\sigma),m_{\sigma,\tau})\in\overline{R}
.

\triangleright

In this final case,
\tau
cannot have the form
\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}
, since only
\mathsf{ok}
and
\mathsf{undef}
states are related to
\mathsf{ok}
states according to
R
, and we have already handled both of those cases. This means that
\sigma
must also not be an
\mathsf{ok}
state, since
\mathsf{ok}
states are only related to other
\mathsf{ok}
states. Therefore,
f^{\prime}(\sigma)=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\sigma)
and
f^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tau)
. By
Lemma

B.6
, we conclude that
(\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\sigma),\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tau))\in\overline{R}
.

Given this, we know that for each
\sigma
and
\tau
, there must be some
m_{\sigma,\tau}
and
m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}
such that
g^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathsf{prune}(m_{\sigma,\tau})
and:

f^{\prime}(\sigma)=\lambda\sigma^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{\sigma,\tau})}m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\qquad m_{\sigma,\tau}=\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(f^{\prime}(\sigma))}m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

Since
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\overline{R}
, we know that there is an
m
such that
m_{1}=\lambda\sigma.{\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}}m(\sigma,\tau)
and
m_{2}=\lambda\tau.{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}}m(\sigma,\tau)

Now let:

m^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\qquad\qquad m^{\prime}_{2}(\tau^{\prime})=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f))}m^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

Now, we show that:

\displaystyle\lambda\sigma^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})}m^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lambda\sigma^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lambda\sigma^{\prime}.\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})}m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

Now,
\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime}_{2})
is all those
\tau^{\prime}
such that
m^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\neq\dsser{0}
for some
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f))
, which also means that
m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\neq\dsser{0}
.
Since the outer sum is over
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})
, the terms we are summing over (
m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
) will be
\dsser{0}
unless
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(f^{\prime}(\sigma))
.
So, the last sum is equivalent to summing over
\tau^{\prime}
such that there is a
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(f^{\prime}(\sigma))
such that
m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\neq\dsser{0}
, which is exactly
\mathsf{supp}(m_{\sigma,\tau})
.

\displaystyle=\lambda\sigma^{\prime}.\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot(\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{\sigma,\tau})}m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\lambda\sigma^{\prime}.\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m_{1}(\sigma)\cdot f^{\prime}(\sigma)(\sigma^{\prime})=\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f)

So
(\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f),m^{\prime}_{2})\in\overline{R}
. It now just remains to prove that
\mathsf{bind}(m_{2},g)=\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{2})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{prune}(\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f))}m^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{prune}(\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f))}\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{prune}(\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot\;\;\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{bind}(m_{1},f))}m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{prune}(\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot\;\;\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(f^{\prime}(\sigma))}m^{\prime}_{\sigma,\tau}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{prune}(\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot m_{\sigma,\tau}(\tau^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot\mathsf{prune}(m_{\sigma,\tau}(\tau^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m(\sigma,\tau)\cdot g^{\prime}(\tau)(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lambda\tau^{\prime}.\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m_{2}(\tau)\cdot g^{\prime}(\tau)(\tau^{\prime})=\mathsf{bind}(m_{2},g)

∎

Lemma C.6.

Let
R=\mathsf{Rep}\circ\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})
.
If
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s[X\mapsto n],h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime}))\in R
and
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s_{1},h_{1}),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s_{2},h_{2}))\in R
whenever
h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s))\in\mathsf{Val}
, then

(\mathsf{update}(s[X\mapsto n],h,\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s[X\mapsto n]),s_{1},h_{1}),\mathsf{update}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime},\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s),s_{2},h_{2}))\in\overline{R}

Proof.

Let
\ell=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s[X\mapsto n])
(since
X
cannot affect the program expression
e
). We complete the proof by case analysis:

\triangleright

h(\ell)\in\mathsf{Val}
. It must also be the case that
(h\uplus h^{\prime})(\ell)\in\mathsf{Val}
, since
h^{\prime}
is disjoint from
h
.
So, it just remains to show that
(\mathsf{unit}(s_{1},h_{1}),\mathsf{unit}(s_{2},h_{2}))\in\overline{R}
, which follows from
Lemma

B.6
.

\triangleright

h(\ell)=\bot
. By a similar argument to the previous case, it must be that
(h\uplus h^{\prime})(\ell)=\bot
too. So, we just need to show that
(\mathsf{error}(s[X\mapsto n],h),\mathsf{error}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime}))\in\overline{R}
. We know that
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{er}}(s[X\mapsto n],h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{er}}(s[X\mapsto n],h\uplus h^{\prime}))\in R
since
R
treats
\mathsf{ok}
and
\mathsf{er}
the same, so the claim follows by
Lemma

B.6
.

\triangleright

\ell\notin\mathsf{dom}(h)
. So,
\mathsf{update}(s[X\mapsto n],h,\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s),s_{1},h_{1})=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathsf{undef})
, and since
R
relates
\mathsf{undef}
to all states,
(\mathsf{update}(s,h,\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s),s_{1},h_{1}),m)\in\overline{R}
for all
m
, which means that
\mathsf{update}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime},\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s),s_{2},h_{2})
is related trivially.

∎

Lemma C.7 (The Frame Property).

Let
R=\mathsf{Rep}\circ\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})
, so
R\subseteq\mathsf{St}\times(\mathsf{St}\cup\{\lightning\})
.
For any program
C
such that
\mathsf{mod}(C)\cap\mathsf{fv}(F)=\emptyset
:

\forall(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}))\in R.\quad\exists m.\quad\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{prune}(m)\quad\text{and}\quad(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),m)\in\overline{R}

Proof.

By induction on the structure of the program
C
.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{skip}
. In this case,
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h)=\mathsf{unit}(s,h)
and
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, so the claim follows from
Lemma

B.6
.

\triangleright

C=C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}
. By definition, we know that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h)=\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}})
and
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}})
.
By the induction hypothesis, we know there is some
m
such that
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{prune}(m)
and
(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),m)\in\overline{R}
.
Therefore by the induction hypothesis and
Lemma

C.5
, we can conclude that there is some
m^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}})=\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})
and
(\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}),m^{\prime})\in\overline{R}
, which completes the proof.

\triangleright

C=C_{1}+C_{2}
. We know that
\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h)=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h)+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h)
and that
\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
.
By the induction hypothesis, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
we get that there is some
m_{i}
such that
\left\llbracket C_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{prune}(m_{i})
and
(\left\llbracket C_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),m_{i})\in\overline{R}
. Using
Lemma

B.4
we can conclude that
(\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),m_{1}+m_{2})\in\overline{R}
. It now only remains to show that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{prune}(m_{1}+m_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{prune}(m_{1})+\mathsf{prune}(m_{2})

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

We now move on to the cases involving state.
Since
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}))\in R
, then there must be some
n
and
h^{\prime\prime}
such that
s=s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n]
,
h^{\prime}=h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}
,
(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})\vDash F
, and for any
s_{0}
and
h_{0}
, if
s_{0}(X)=n
, then
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s_{0},h_{0})=\mathsf{alloc}(s_{0}[X\mapsto s^{\prime}(X)],h_{0}\uplus h^{\prime\prime})
.
Additionally, many of the cases have a single outcome, so by
Lemma

B.6
it suffices to show that
(\sigma,\tau)\in R
where
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h)=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\sigma)
and
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tau)
in those cases.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{assume}~e
. Since
s
and
s^{\prime}
only differ in the value of
X
, which cannot affect the value of
e
, then
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)
. This means that both programs weight the computation by the same amount, let this weight be
a=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)
. So, we get that
\left\llbracket\mathsf{assume}~e\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h)=a\cdot\mathsf{unit}(s,h)
and
\left\llbracket\mathsf{assume}~e\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=a\cdot\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
. Since
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}))\in R
, then by
Lemma

B.6

(\mathsf{unit}(s,h),\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}))\in\overline{R}
, so by
Lemma

B.5

(a\cdot\mathsf{unit}(s,h),a\cdot\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}))\in\overline{R}
.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C^{\prime}
. First, we will show that there exists
m
such that
F_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}^{n}(\bot)(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{prune}(m)
and
(F_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}\rangle}^{n}(\bot)(s,h),m)\in\overline{R}
for any
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}))\in R
. The proof is by induction on
n
. If
n=0
, then the claim holds using
Lemma

B.2
:

F^{0}_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}(\bot)(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\bot(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\dsser{0}=\bot(s,h)=F^{0}_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}\rangle}(\bot)(s,h)

Now suppose the claim holds for
n
, we will show that it also holds for
n+1
:

\displaystyle F_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}^{n+1}(\bot)(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}),F_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}^{n}(\bot))&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})=\dsser{1}\\
\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})=\dsser{0}\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle F_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}\rangle}^{n+1}(\bot)(s,h)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{bind}(\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),F_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}\rangle}^{n}(\bot))&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{1}\\
\mathsf{unit}(s,h)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{0}\end{array}\right.

Note that as we showed in the previous case for assume,
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})
, so both executions will take the same path.
If
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)=\dsser{1}
, then the claim holds by
Lemma

C.5
and the induction hypothesis. In the second case, it holds by
Lemma

B.6
.

Now, by the definition of
\mathsf{prune}
, this also means that for any
n
, there exists
a_{n}
such that:

(F^{n}_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}\rangle}(\bot)(s,h),F^{n}_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}(\bot)(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})+a_{n}\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\lightning))\in\overline{R}

Recall that
\lightning
represents the nonterminating traces, and as we continue to unroll the loop, the weight of nontermination can only increase, so the
a_{n}
must increase monotonically and therefore
F^{n}_{\langle C^{\prime},e,\mathsf{alloc}\rangle}(\bot)(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})+a_{n}\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\bot)
is a chain, so
by
Lemma

B.8
we know that:

(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F^{n}_{\langle C^{\prime},e\rangle}(\bot)(s,h),\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F^{n}_{\langle C^{\prime},e\rangle}(\bot)(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})+a_{n}\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\lightning))\in\overline{R}

And we can therefore conclude that there exists
m
such that
\left\llbracket\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\mathsf{prune}(m)
and
(\left\llbracket\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(s,h),m)\in\overline{R}
.

\triangleright

C=(x\coloneqq e)
. We know the following:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n,x\mapsto\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n])],h)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})][X\mapsto n],h)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})],h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})

Since
x\in\mathsf{mod}(C)
, then
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(F)
, so updating
x
in
s
will not affect the truth of
F
, therefore
(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})],h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})\vDash F
. In addition, since we did not modify the value of
X
, it is still true that
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s_{0},h_{0})=\mathsf{alloc}(s_{0}[X\mapsto s^{\prime}(X)],h_{0}\uplus h^{\prime\prime})
for any
s_{0},h_{0}
with
s_{0}(X)=n
. So, we know that:

(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})][X\mapsto n],h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s[x\mapsto\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})],h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})

Putting this together along with the fact that
\mathsf{Rep}
is reflexive finishes the proof.

\triangleright

C=(x\coloneqq\mathsf{alloc}())
. We know the following:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h),\lambda(\ell,v).\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n][x\mapsto\ell],h[\ell\mapsto v]))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathsf{alloc}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}),\lambda(\ell,v).\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto\ell][X\mapsto n],h[\ell\mapsto v]))

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathsf{alloc}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}),\lambda(\ell,v).\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto\ell],(h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})[\ell\mapsto v]))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathsf{alloc}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}),\lambda(\ell,v).\mathsf{unit}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto\ell],h[\ell\mapsto v]\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))

So it is clear that
(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}(s,h),\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime}))\in\overline{R}
since the two weighting functions are identical except that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})
has
h^{\prime\prime}
added at every state and just as in the previous case, we did not update
X
, so the relationship between
\mathsf{alloc}
and
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}
holds as well.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{free}(e)
. Using
Lemma

C.6
, we only need to show that if
h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))\in\mathsf{Val}
, then:

(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h[\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)\mapsto\bot]),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},(h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})[\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\bot]))\in\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})\subseteq R

If
h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))\in\mathsf{Val}
, then
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\in\mathsf{dom}(h)
, and since
h^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from
h
, then
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\notin\mathsf{dom}(h^{\prime\prime})
, so

(h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})[\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\bot]=h[\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\bot]\uplus h^{\prime\prime}
, and clearly:

(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h[\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\bot]),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},h[\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\bot])\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})

\triangleright

C=([e_{1}]\leftarrow e_{2})
. Using
Lemma

C.6
, we only need to show that if
h(\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))\in\mathsf{Val}
, then:

\displaystyle(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h[\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},(h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})[\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]))

\displaystyle\quad\in\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\subseteq R

If
h(\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))\in\mathsf{Val}
, then
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\in\mathsf{dom}(h)
, and since
h^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from
h
, then
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\notin\mathsf{dom}(h^{\prime\prime})
, so

(h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})[\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]=h)[\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]\uplus h^{\prime\prime}
. Now, clearly:

(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h[\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime},h[\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\mapsto\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\in R

\triangleright

C=(x\leftarrow[e])
.
Using
Lemma

C.6
, we only need to show that if
h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))\in\mathsf{Val}
, then:

(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s[X\mapsto n][x\mapsto h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})),h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto(h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]),h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\in R

If
h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))\in\mathsf{Val}
, then
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\in\mathsf{dom}(h)
, and since
h^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from
h
, then
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})\notin\mathsf{dom}(h^{\prime\prime})
, so
s^{\prime}[x\mapsto(h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))]=s^{\prime}[x\mapsto h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))]
.

So, clearly
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s)][X\mapsto n]),h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[x\mapsto h(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime}))]),h\uplus h^{\prime\prime})\in R
.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{error}()
. By
Lemma

B.6
, it suffices to show that
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{er}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n],h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{er}}(s^{\prime},h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\in R
, which follows from the assumptions since
R
treats
\mathsf{ok}
and
\mathsf{er}
states in the same way.

\triangleright

C=f(\vec{e})
. Let
C^{\prime}
be the body of
f
. By the same argument used in the assignment case,
(\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[X\mapsto n][\vec{x}\mapsto\left\llbracket\vec{e}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})],h),\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s^{\prime}[\vec{x}\mapsto\left\llbracket\vec{e}\right\rrbracket(s^{\prime})]),h\uplus h^{\prime\prime}))\in R
. So, the claim follows from the induction hypothesis.

∎

See
4.6

Proof.

Let
R=\mathsf{Rep}\circ\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})
.
Suppose
m\vDash\varphi\circledast F
, take any
\mathsf{alloc}\in\mathsf{Alloc}
, and pick some
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi,\psi,F)
. Then by
Lemmas

4.4
and
C.4
we know that there are
m_{1}
,
m^{\prime}_{1}
,
m_{2}
, and
\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}
such that
(m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})}
and
m=m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}
and
m_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}\vDash\varphi
for any
m_{2}^{\prime}
such that
|m_{2}^{\prime}|\leq|m_{2}|
. So that means that
m_{1}+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef})\vDash\varphi
. We know that:

\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}\!\!(m_{1}+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef}))=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}\!\!(m_{1})+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef})

So, therefore
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}\!\!(m_{1})+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef})\vDash\psi
since
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
.
Now, observe that
(m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})}\subseteq\overline{R}
since
\mathsf{Rep}
is reflexive.
We also know that
(|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef}),m_{2})\in\overline{R}
since
R
permits
\mathsf{undef}
states to be remapped to anything. Therefore, using
Lemma

B.4
we get that:

(m_{1}+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef}),m)=(m_{1}+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef}),m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2})\in\overline{R}

So, using
Lemmas

C.5
and
C.7
, we know that there exists some
m^{\prime}
such that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=\mathsf{prune}(m^{\prime})
and:

(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}\!\!(m_{1}+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef})),m^{\prime})\in\overline{R}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{Rep}}\circ\overline{\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})}

Where the last step is by
Lemma

B.7
.
All that remains now is to peel away the layers in the above expression. More concretely, we know that there is some
m^{\prime\prime}
such that
(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime}}\!\!(m_{1}+|m_{2}|\cdot\mathsf{unit}(\mathsf{undef})),m^{\prime\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{frame}^{\prime}(F,X,\mathsf{alloc},\mathsf{alloc}^{\prime})}
and
(m^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathsf{Rep}}
. By
Lemma

C.3

m^{\prime\prime}\vDash\psi\circledast F
and by
Lemma

4.5
,
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi\circledast F
.

∎

Appendix D
Tri-Abduction

The full set of inference rules for the tri-abduction proof system is shown in
Figure

6
. The abduction algorithm is given in
Algorithm

1
.

Algorithm 1

abduce-par(
P
,
Q
)

1:

if
either
Base-Emp
,
Base-True-L
, or
Base-True-R
apply
then

2:

return
anti-frame
M
as indicated by that rule

3:

else

4:

for
Each remaining row in
Figure

6
from top to bottom
do

5:

\text{result}=\emptyset

6:

for
Each inference rule in the row of the form below
do

\frac{P^{\prime}\lhd[M^{\prime}]\rhd Q^{\prime}\quad R}{P\lhd[M]\rhd Q}

7:

if
The input parameters match
P
and
Q
in the inference rule and
R
is true
then

8:

\text{result}=\text{result}\cup\{M\mid M^{\prime}\in\textsf{abduce-par}(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\}

9:

end

if

10:

end

for

11:

if

\text{result}\neq\emptyset

then

12:

return

result

13:

end

if

14:

end

for

15:

return

\emptyset

16:

end

if

Base Cases

\frac{\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\not\vdash\mathsf{false}}{\Pi\land\mathsf{emp}\lhd[\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\land\mathsf{emp}]\rhd\Pi^{\prime}\land\mathsf{emp}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Base-Emp}

\frac{\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}\not\vdash\mathsf{false}}{\Pi\land\mathsf{true}\lhd[\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}]\rhd\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Base-True-L}\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\wedge\Sigma\not\vdash\mathsf{false}}{\Pi\wedge\Sigma\lhd[\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\wedge\Sigma]\rhd\Pi^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{true}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Base-True-R}

Quantifier Elimination

\frac{\Delta\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\vec{X}\cap(\mathsf{fv}(\Delta^{\prime})\setminus\vec{Y})=\emptyset\hskip 8.50012pt\vec{Y}\cap(\mathsf{fv}(\Delta)\setminus\vec{X})=\emptyset}{\exists\vec{X}.\Delta\lhd[\exists\vec{X}\vec{Y}.M]\rhd\exists\vec{Y}.\Delta^{\prime}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Exists}

Resource Matching

\frac{\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}}{\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})\lhd[M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Ls-Start-L}\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{\Delta\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})}{\Delta\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}\lhd[M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Ls-Start-R}

\frac{\Delta\wedge e_{2}=e_{3}\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\wedge e_{2}=e_{3}}{\Delta\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\lhd[M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Match}

\frac{\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}}{\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})\lhd[M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Ls-End-L}\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{\Delta\lhd[M]\rhd\mathsf{ls}(e_{2},e_{3})\ast\Delta^{\prime}}{\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})\lhd[M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Ls-End-R}

Resource Adding

\frac{\Delta\lhd[M]\rhd\Pi^{\prime}\land(\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true})\hskip 8.50012pt\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\not\vdash\mathsf{false}}{\Delta\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\lhd[M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})]\rhd\Pi^{\prime}\land(\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Missing-L}

\frac{\Pi\land(\Sigma\ast\mathsf{true})\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\Pi\land\Sigma\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\not\vdash\mathsf{false}}{\Pi\land(\Sigma\ast\mathsf{true})\lhd[M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Missing-R}

\frac{\Delta\land e_{1}=e_{2}\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\land e_{1}=e_{2}}{\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Emp-Ls-L}\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{\Delta\land e_{1}=e_{2}\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\land e_{1}=e_{2}}{\Delta\lhd[M]\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rightstyle={\footnotesize\sc},right=Emp-Ls-R}

Figure 6.
Tri-abduction proof rules. Similarly to
Calcagno et al. 2009
, in the above we use
B(e_{1},e_{2})
to represent either
\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
or
e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}
.

Lemma D.1.

If
P\lhd[M]\rhd Q
is derivable, then
M\vDash P
and
M\vDash Q

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation of
P\lhd[M]\rhd Q
.

(1)

Base-Emp
.

We need to show that
\Pi\land\Pi^{\prime}\land\mathsf{emp}\vDash\Pi\land\mathsf{emp}
and
\Pi\land\Pi^{\prime}\land\mathsf{emp}\vDash\Pi^{\prime}\land\mathsf{emp}
, both hold by the semantics of logical conjunction.

(2)

Base-True-L
.

We need to show that
\Pi\land\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}\vDash\Pi\land\mathsf{true}
and
\Pi\land\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}\vDash\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}
, both hold by the semantics of logical conjunction.

(3)

Base-True-R
.
This case is symmetric to Case
2
.

(4)

Exists
.

Here, we know that
M\vDash\Delta
and
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}
. We also know that
\vec{X}
is not free in
\Delta^{\prime}
with
\vec{Y}
removed and vice versa. Now let:

\vec{Z}=\vec{X}\cap\vec{Y}\qquad\vec{X}^{\prime}=\vec{X}\setminus\vec{Z}\qquad\vec{Y}^{\prime}=\vec{Y}\setminus\vec{Z}

That is,
\vec{Z}
is the variables occurring in both
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
,
\vec{X}^{\prime}
is the variables only occurring in
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}^{\prime}
is the variables only occurring in
\vec{Y}
. This means that
\vec{X}^{\prime}
,
\vec{Y}^{\prime}
, and
\vec{Z}
are disjoint.

We first show that
\exists\vec{X}\vec{Y}.M\vDash\exists\vec{X}.\Delta
. Suppose
(s,h)\vDash\exists\vec{X}\vec{Y}.M
. We know that
(s^{\prime},h)\vDash M
where
s^{\prime}=s[\vec{X}^{\prime}\mapsto\vec{v}_{1}][\vec{Y}^{\prime}\mapsto\vec{v}_{2}][\vec{Z}\mapsto v_{3}]
for some
\vec{v}_{1},\vec{v}_{2}
and
\vec{v}_{3}
.
Given that we know
M\vDash\Delta
, we now have
(s^{\prime},h)\vDash\Delta
and therefore
(s[Y^{\prime}\mapsto\vec{v}_{2}],h)\vDash\exists\vec{X}.\Delta
(since
\vec{X}=\vec{X}^{\prime}\cup\vec{Z}
). Now, given that
\vec{Y}\cap(\mathsf{fv}(\Delta)\setminus\vec{X})=\emptyset
, we know that none of the variables in
\vec{Y}^{\prime}
are free in
\Delta
, and so we can remove them from the state to conclude that
(s,h)\vDash\exists\vec{X}.\Delta
.

It can also be shown that
\exists\vec{X}\vec{Y}.M\vDash\exists\vec{Y}.\Delta^{\prime}
by a symmetric argument.

(5)

Ls-Start-L
.
Here, we know
M\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})
and
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}
. We now want to show
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
and
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
.

Suppose that
(s,h)\vDash M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
, and so
(s,h_{1})\vDash M
and
(s,h_{2})\vDash e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
for some
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
such that
h=h_{1}\uplus h_{2}
.

Since
M\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})
, we get that
(s,h_{1})\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})
and recombining, we get that
(s,h)\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
. Now,
\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}\vDash\exists X.e_{1}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{ls}(X,e_{2})
and
\exists X.e_{1}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{ls}(X,e_{2})\vDash\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
, so we get that
(s,h)\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
and therefore
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
.

We also know that
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}
which means that because
(s,h_{1})\vDash M
,
(s,h_{1})\vDash\Delta^{\prime}
. This means that
(s,h_{1}\uplus h_{2})\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
and
h=h_{1}\uplus h_{2}
, so we now have
(s,h)\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
, therefore
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}

(6)

Ls-Start-R
.
This case is symmetric to Case
5
.

(7)

Match
.

Here, we know that
M\vDash\Delta\land e_{2}=e_{3}
and
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\land e_{2}=e_{3}
. We want to show that
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\vDash\Delta\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}
and
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
.

Let us first show
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\vDash\Delta\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}
. Because we know that
M\vDash\Delta
and
e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\vDash e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}
, it follows that
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\vDash\Delta\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}
.

Let us now show
M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
. We know
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\land e_{2}=e_{3}
, now suppose that
(s,h)\vDash M\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}
, so
(s,h)\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\land e_{2}=e_{3}
as well. This means that
e_{2}=e_{3}
in state
s
, and therefore it must also be the case that
(s,h)\vDash e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
. Given what else we know, we conclude that
(s,h)\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
.

(8)

Ls-End-L
.

Here, we know that
M\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})
and
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}
. We want to show that
M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
and
M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})
.

Let us first show
M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
.
First, we know that
M\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})
, and so
M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})
. Clearly, it is also the case that
\mathsf{ls}(e_{3},e_{2})\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
, and so combining these facts we get
M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
.

Let us now show
M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})
. We know that
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}
, so clearly
M\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{3})
.

(9)

Ls-End-R
.

This case is symmetric to Case
8
.

(10)

Missing-L
.

Here, we know that
M\vDash\Delta
and
M\vDash\Pi^{\prime}\land(\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true})
. This means we also know that
M\vDash\Pi^{\prime}
and
M\vDash\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true}
by semantic definition.

Let us first show that
M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\vDash\Delta\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})
. Suppose that
(s,h)\vDash M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})
.
We know that
(s,h_{1})\vDash M
and
(s,h_{2})\vDash B(e_{1},e_{2})
for some
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
such that
h=h_{1}\uplus h_{2}
. Since
M\vDash\Delta
, then
(s,h_{1})\vDash\Delta
. This means that
(s,h)\vDash\Delta\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})
, therefore
M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\vDash\Delta\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})
.

Let us now show that
M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\vDash\Pi^{\prime}\ast(\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true})
. Here, we know that
M\vDash\Pi^{\prime}\ast(\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true})
and trivially
B(e_{1},e_{2})\vDash\mathsf{true}
. This means
M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\vDash\Pi^{\prime}\ast(\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true})\ast\mathsf{true}
; therefore,
M\ast B(e_{1},e_{2})\vDash\Pi^{\prime}\ast(\Sigma^{\prime}\ast\mathsf{true})
.

(11)

Missing-R
.

This case is symmetric to Case
10
.

(12)

Emp-Ls-L
.

We know that
M\vDash\Delta\land e_{1}=e_{2}
and
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}\land e_{1}=e_{2}
. This means that
M\vDash\Delta^{\prime}
, so the right side of the tri-abductive judgement is taken care of.

Let us now show
M\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
. We first establish that
e_{1}=e_{2}\land\mathsf{emp}\vDash\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
by definition, since
\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})\iff(\mathsf{emp}\land e_{1}=e_{2})\vee\exists X.e_{1}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{ls}(X,e_{2})
. Now, we know that
M\vDash\Delta\land e_{1}=e_{2}
, which also means that
M\vDash(\Delta\ast\mathsf{emp})\land e_{1}=e_{2}
, or equivalently,
M\vDash(\Delta\land e_{1}=e_{2})\ast(\mathsf{emp}\land e_{1}=e_{2})
. Using
e_{1}=e_{2}\land\mathsf{emp}\vDash\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
, we get
M\vDash(\Delta\land e_{1}=e_{2})\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})
, and by weakening we get
M\vDash\Delta\ast\mathsf{ls}(e_{1},e_{2})

(13)

Emp-Ls-R
.
This case is symmetric to Case
12

∎

See
5.1

Proof.

In our tri-abduction algorithm, we call
abduce-par
on
P\ast\mathsf{true}
and
Q\ast\mathsf{true}
, so we know based on
Lemma

D.1
that if
P\ast\mathsf{true}\lhd[M]\rhd Q\ast\mathsf{true}
is derivable, then
M\vDash P\ast\mathsf{true}
and
M\vDash Q\ast\mathsf{true}
since
abduce-par
operates by applying the inference in
Figure

3
. The procedure for finding
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
follows that of
Berdine
et al. 2005b
and so
M\vDash P\ast F_{1}
and
M\vDash Q\ast F_{2}
by
Berdine
et al. 2005b
.
∎

Appendix E
Symbolic Execution

E.1.
Renaming

We first define the renaming procedure in
Algorithm

2
, which is identical to that of
Calcagno et al. 2011
except that we additionally require
\vec{e}
to be disjoint from
\vec{x}
. Renaming produces a new anti-frame
M_{0}
which is similar to
M
except that it is guaranteed not to mention any program variables and so it trivially meets the side condition of the frame rule. It additionally provides a vector of expressions
\vec{e}
to be substituted for the free variables in the postcondition
\vec{Y}
so as to match
M_{0}
.

Algorithm 2

\mathsf{rename}(\Delta,M,Q,\mathcal{Q},\vec{X},\vec{x})

Let
\vec{Y}
be the free logical variables of
Q
and all the assertions in
\mathcal{Q}
.

Pick
\vec{e}
disjoint from
\vec{Y}
and
\vec{x}
such that
\Delta\ast M\vDash\vec{e}=\vec{Y}
.

Pick
M^{\prime}
disjoint from
\vec{X}
,
\vec{Y}
, and
\mathsf{Var}
such that
\Delta\ast M^{\prime}\vDash\Delta\ast M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]
.

return

(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})

Now, we recall the definitions of the following two procedures:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}(\exists\vec{Z}.\Delta,Q,\psi,\vec{x})=\\
\quad\big\{(M^{\prime},(\psi\circledast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}])\\
\quad\mid(M,F)\in\mathsf{biab}(\Delta,Q)\\
\quad\quad(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})=\mathsf{rename}(\Delta,M,Q,\{\psi\},\vec{Z})\big\}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(P_{1},P_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})=\\
\quad\big\{(M^{\prime},(\psi_{1}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{1}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}],\\
\quad\phantom{\big\{(M^{\prime},\,}(\psi_{2}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{2}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}])\\
\quad\quad~\mid(M,F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}(P_{1},P_{2})\\
\quad\quad(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})=\mathsf{rename}(\mathsf{emp},M,\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\},\emptyset)\big\}\end{array}

The
\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}
procedure is similar to
AbduceAndAdapt
from
Calcagno et al. 2011
. Since the bi-abduction procedure does not support existentially quantified assertions on the left hand side, the existentials must be stripped and then re-added later (as is also done in
Calcagno et al. 2011
). The renaming step ensures that the anti-frame
M^{\prime}
is safe to use with the frame rule. We capture the motivation behind
\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}
using the following correctness lemma, stating that
\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}
produces a suitable frame and anti-frame so as to adapt a specification
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:Q\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
to use a different precondition
P
.

Lemma E.1.

For all
(M,\psi^{\prime})\in\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}(P,Q,\psi,\vec{x})
, if
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:Q\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
and
\vec{x}=\mathsf{mod}(C)
, then

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\ast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}\rangle}

Proof.

By definition, any element of
\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}(P,Q,\psi,\vec{x})
(where
P=\exists\vec{Z}.\Delta
) must have the form
(M^{\prime},(\psi\circledast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}])
where
(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})=\mathsf{rename}(\Delta,M,Q,\{\psi\},\vec{X})
and
(M,F)\in\mathsf{biab}(\Delta,Q)
.
By the definition of rename, we know that:

\displaystyle\Delta\ast M^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vDash\Delta\ast M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

Since
M^{\prime}
is assumed to be disjoint from
\vec{Z}
, then
\exists\vec{Z}.M^{\prime}
iff
M^{\prime}
, so we can existentially quantify both sides to obtain:

(1)

\displaystyle P\ast M^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vDash\exists\vec{Z}.\Delta\ast M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

In addition,
(M,F)\in\mathsf{biab}(\Delta,Q)
, so we also know that:

\displaystyle\Delta\ast M

\displaystyle\vDash Q\ast F

In
Figure

5
, we assumed all the logical variables used are fresh, so
\Delta
must be disjoint from
\vec{Y}
(the free variables of
Q
and
\psi
), and therefore
\Delta[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]=\Delta
, so substituting both sides, we get:

\displaystyle\Delta\ast M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

\displaystyle\vDash(Q\ast F)[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

We also weaken the right hand side by replacing
\vec{x}
with fresh existentially quantified variables in
F
.

\displaystyle\Delta\ast M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

\displaystyle\vDash(Q\ast\exists\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

Now, we can existentially quantify both sides of the entailment. Since logical variables are fresh,
\vec{Z}
is disjoint from
Q
.

(2)

\displaystyle\exists\vec{Z}.\Delta\ast M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

\displaystyle\vDash(Q\ast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

And finally, we combine
Equations

1
and
2
to get:

(3)

\displaystyle P\ast M^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vDash(Q\ast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

Now, given that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:Q\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
, we can use the frame rule to get:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:Q\ast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\circledast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]\rangle}

This is clearly valid, since
\vec{x}=\mathsf{mod}(C)
has been removed from the assertion that we are framing in, therefore satisfying
\mathsf{mod}(C)\cap\mathsf{fv}(\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])=\emptyset
.
We can also substitute
\vec{e}
for
\vec{Y}
in the pre- and postconditions since we assumed that
\vec{e}
is disjoint from the program variables
\vec{x}
, and therefore
\vec{e}
remains constant after executing
C
.

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:(Q\ast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\psi\circledast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}

Finally, using the rule of consequence with
Equation

3
, we strengthen the precondition to get:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\ast M^{\prime}\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\psi\circledast\exists\vec{Z}\vec{X}.F[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}

∎

The
\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}
procedure is similar, but it is fundamentally based on tri-abduction and is accordingly used for parallel composition instead of sequential composition. We include two separate proofs corresponding to the two ways in which tri-abduction is using during symbolic execution. The first (
Lemma

E.2
) pertains to merging the anti-frames obtained by continuing to evaluate a single program
C
after the control flow has already branched whereas the second (
Lemma

E.3
) deals with merging the preconditions from two different program program branches,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.

Lemma E.2.

If
(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1},\psi^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{1}\circledast M_{1}\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{1}\rangle}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{2}\circledast M_{2}\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{2}\rangle}
and
\vec{x}=\mathsf{mod}(C)
, then
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{1}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{2}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{2}\rangle}
.

Proof.

By definition, any element of
\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})
will have the form:

\left(M^{\prime},(\psi_{1}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{1}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}],(\psi_{2}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{2}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\right)

Where
(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})=\mathsf{rename}(\mathsf{emp},M,\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\},\emptyset,\vec{x})
and
(M,F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}(P_{1},P_{2})
. From rename, we know that
M^{\prime}\vDash M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]
and from tri-abduction, we know that
M\vDash M_{i}\ast F_{i}
for
i=1,2
, so
M^{\prime}\vDash(M_{i}\ast F_{i})[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]
. We can weaken this by replacing
\vec{x}
in
F_{i}
with fresh existentially quantified variables to obtain
M^{\prime}\vDash(M_{i}\ast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]
.
By assumption, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{i}\circledast M_{i}\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{i}\rangle}
for
i=1,2
. So, using the frame rule, we get:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{i}\circledast(M_{i}\ast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{i}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]\rangle}

This is valid since
\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]
is disjoint from
\vec{x}
(the modified program variables) by construction.
We also assumed in
rename
that
\vec{e}
is disjoint from
\vec{x}
, so we can substitute
\vec{e}
for
\vec{Y}
to get:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\varphi_{i}\circledast(M_{i}\ast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]))[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\psi_{i}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]i)[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}

Note that
\varphi_{i}[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]=\varphi_{i}
, since the logical variables
\vec{Y}
are generated freshly, independent of
\varphi_{i}
, as was mentioned in
Figure

5
. So, using the rule of consequence we get:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{i}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\psi_{i}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}

∎

Lemma E.3.

If
(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1},\psi^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(P_{1},P_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P_{1}\rangle}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{1}\rangle}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P_{2}\rangle}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{2}\rangle}
and
\vec{x}=\mathsf{mod}(C_{1},C_{2})
, then
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M\rangle}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M\rangle}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{2}\rangle}
.

Proof.

By definition, any element of
\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(P_{1},P_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})
will have the form:

\left(M^{\prime},(\psi_{1}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{1}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}],(\psi_{2}\circledast\exists\vec{X}.F_{2}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\right)

Where
(\vec{e},\vec{Y},M^{\prime})=\mathsf{rename}(\mathsf{emp},M,\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\},\emptyset,\vec{x})
and
(M,F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}(P_{1},P_{2})
. From rename, we know that
M^{\prime}\vDash M[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]
and from tri-abduction, we know that
M\vDash P_{i}\ast F_{i}
for
i=1,2
, so
M^{\prime}\vDash(P_{i}\ast F_{i})[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]
. We can weaken this by replacing
\vec{x}
in
F_{i}
with fresh existentially quantified variables to obtain
M^{\prime}\vDash(P_{i}\ast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]

By the frame rule, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P_{i}\ast\exists\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]\rangle}~C_{i}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{i}\circledast\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]\rangle}
since
\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}]
must be disjoint from the modified program variables
\vec{x}
.
By substituting into both the pre and postconditions, we get
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\mathsf{ok}:P_{i}\ast\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}~C_{i}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\psi_{i}\circledast\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}
(this is valid since
rename
guarantees that
\vec{e}
is disjoint from
\vec{x}
). Finally, we complete the proof by applying the rule of consequence with
M^{\prime}\vDash(P_{i}\ast\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]
to obtain:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M^{\prime}\rangle}~C_{i}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\psi_{i}\circledast\vec{X}.F_{i}[\vec{X}/\vec{x}])[\vec{e}/\vec{Y}]\rangle}

∎

E.2.
Sequencing Proof

See
5.3

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\top
. We need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\top\circledast\mathsf{emp}\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\top\rangle}
holds. This triple is clearly valid since any triple with the postcondition
\top
is trivially true.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\bowtie\varphi_{2}
where
\mathord{\bowtie}\in\{\vee,\oplus\}
. We need to show:

\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle(\varphi_{1}\bowtie\varphi_{2})\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{1}\bowtie\psi^{\prime}_{2}\rangle}

Where
(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1},\psi^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\vec{x})
and
(M_{i},\psi_{i})\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi_{i},S,\vec{x})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.
By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{i}\circledast M_{i}\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{i}\rangle}
, so by
Lemma

E.2
we get that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{i}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{i}\rangle}
.
We now complete the proof separately for the two logical operators:

–

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
. Suppose that
m\vDash(\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2})\circledast M
, so
m\vDash\varphi_{i}\circledast M
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
. Since
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{i}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{i}\rangle}
, we know that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi^{\prime}_{i}
, and we can weaken this to conclude that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi^{\prime}_{1}\vee\psi^{\prime}_{2}
.

–

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}
. Suppose that
m\vDash(\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2})\circledast M
, and so there are
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
and
m_{i}\vDash\varphi_{i}\circledast M
for each
i
. Since
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi_{i}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{i}\rangle}
, we know that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m_{i})\vDash\psi^{\prime}_{i}
. We also know that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m_{1}+m_{2})=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m_{1})+\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m_{2})
, and so
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\psi^{\prime}_{2}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\varphi^{\prime(a)}
. We need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi^{\prime(a)}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{(a)}\rangle}
where
(M,\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi^{\prime},S,\vec{x})
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi^{\prime}\circledast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
. Now, suppose that
m\vDash\varphi^{\prime(a)}\circledast M
. If
a=\dsser{0}
, then
m=\dsser{0}
and therefore
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi^{(\dsser{0})}
. If not, then there is some
m^{\prime}
such that
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}\circledast M
and
m=a\cdot m^{\prime}
. So,
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m^{\prime})\vDash\psi^{\prime}
. We also know that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(a\cdot m^{\prime})=a\cdot\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m^{\prime})
, so by the semantics of
(-)^{(a)}
,
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi^{(a)}
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\mathsf{ok}:P
. We need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\ast M\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}\rangle}
where
(M,\psi^{\prime})\in\mathsf{biab}^{\prime}(P,Q,\psi,\vec{x})
and
(Q,\psi)\in S
.
By assumption, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:Q\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
. The remainder of the proof follows directly from
Lemma

E.1
.

\triangleright

\varphi=\mathsf{er}:Q
. We need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{er}:Q\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{er}:Q\rangle}
. This trivially holds since any
m
satisfying
\mathsf{er}:Q
must consist only of
\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{er}}(s,h)
states, and so
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=m
.

∎

E.3.
Symbolic Execution Proofs

Lemma E.4.

Let:

\small f(S)=\begin{array}[]{l}\{(\lnot e\land\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:\lnot e\land\mathsf{emp})\}~\cup\\
\{(M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land e,\psi)\mid(M_{1},\varphi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\mathsf{ok}:e\land\mathsf{emp},\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),\mathsf{mod}(C)),(M_{2},\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,S,\mathsf{mod}(C))\}\end{array}

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(P,\varphi)\in f^{n}(\emptyset)
,
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\rangle}
.

Proof.

By induction on
n
. Suppose
n=0
, then
f^{0}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
, so the claim vacuously holds. Now, suppose the claim holds for
n
, we will show it holds for
n+1
. First, observe that:

\displaystyle f^{n+1}(\emptyset)

\displaystyle=f(f^{n}(\emptyset))

\displaystyle=\{(\lnot e\land\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:\lnot e\land\mathsf{emp})\}~\cup

\displaystyle\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}\{(M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land e,\psi)\mid&(M_{1},\varphi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\mathsf{ok}:e\land\mathsf{emp},\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),\mathsf{mod}(C))\\
&(M_{2},\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,f^{n}(\emptyset),\mathsf{mod}(C))\}\end{array}

So any
(P,\varphi)\in f^{n+1}(\emptyset)
comes from one of the two sets in the above union. Suppose it is in the first, so we need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:\lnot e\land\mathsf{emp}\rangle}~\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:\lnot e\land\mathsf{emp}\rangle}
. This is clearly true, since the loop does not execute in states where
\lnot e
holds and therefore the whole command is equivalent to
\mathsf{skip}
.

Now suppose we are in the second case, so the element has the form
(M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land e,\psi)
where
(M_{1},\varphi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\mathsf{ok}:e\land\mathsf{emp},\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),\mathsf{mod}(C))
and
(M_{2},\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,f^{n}(\emptyset),\mathsf{mod}(C))
. By
Lemma

C.5
, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M_{1}\land e\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\rangle}
and by
Lemma

C.5
and the induction hypothesis, we get
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\circledast M_{2}\rangle}~\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
. Using the frame rule, we also get
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land e\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\circledast M_{2}\rangle}
, and so we can sequence the previous specifications to get
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land e\rangle}~C\fatsemi\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
. Now, since the precondition stipulates that
e
is true, the loop must run for at least one iteration, so for any
m\vDash(\mathsf{ok}:M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land e)
,
\left\llbracket C\fatsemi\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=\left\llbracket\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)
, and so
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M_{1}\ast M_{2}\land e\rangle}~\mathsf{while}~e~\mathsf{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
.
∎

Lemma E.5.

If for every
(s,h)\vDash P
and
\mathsf{alloc}\in\mathsf{Alloc}
, there exists
s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
such that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h)=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime}))
and
(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\vDash Q
, then
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\epsilon:Q\rangle}

Proof.

Suppose that
m\vDash\mathsf{ok}:P
. That means that
|m|=\dsser{1}
and all elements of
\mathsf{supp}(m)
have the form
\dsser{i}_{\mathsf{ok}}(s,h)
where
(s,h)\vDash P
. By assumption, we know that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{alloc}}(s,h)=\mathsf{unit}_{\mathcal{W}}(\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime}))
such that
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\vDash Q
. This means that every element of
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)
must have the form
\dsser{i}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
where
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\vDash Q
and also
|\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)|=\dsser{1}
since each
(s,h)
does not change the mass of the distribution, so
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\epsilon:Q
.
∎

See
5.2

Proof.

By induction on the structure of the program
C
.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{skip}
. We need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp}\rangle}~\mathsf{skip}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp}\rangle}
, which is trivially true.

\triangleright

C=C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}
. By definition, any element of
\left\llbracket C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
must have the form
(P\ast M,\psi)
where
(P,\varphi)\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
and
(M,\psi)\in\mathsf{seq}(\varphi,\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T),\mathsf{mod}(C_{2}))
.
By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\rangle}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\rangle}
and by
Lemma

C.5
we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\circledast M\rangle}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
. Using the frame rule, we get that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\ast M\rangle}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\varphi\circledast M\rangle}
(given the renaming step used in
seq
,
M
contains no program variables, so it must obey the side condition of the frame rule). Finally, we can join the two specifications to conclude that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:P\ast M\rangle}~C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi\rangle}
.

\triangleright

C=C_{1}+C_{2}
. Any element of
\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
must have the form
(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\psi^{\prime}_{2})
where
(M,\psi^{\prime}_{1},\psi^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{triab}^{\prime}(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\mathsf{mod}(C_{1},C_{2}))
and
(M_{1},\psi_{1})\in\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
and
(M_{2},\psi_{2})\in\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M_{i}\rangle}~C_{i}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi_{i}\rangle}
for
i=1,2
. By
Lemma

E.3
we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:M\rangle}~C_{i}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{i}\rangle}
. Now, we show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle M\rangle}~C_{1}+C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\psi^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\psi^{\prime}_{2}\rangle}
: suppose
m\vDash M
. By definition,
\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)
. Now, using what we obtained from
Lemma

E.3
, we know that since
m\vDash M
,
\left\llbracket C_{i}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Combining these two, we get that
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)\vDash\psi^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\psi^{\prime}_{2}
.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{assume}~b
. Any element of
\left\llbracket\mathsf{assume}~b\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
must have the form
(b\land\mathsf{emp},\mathsf{ok}:b\land\mathsf{emp})
or
(\lnot b\land\mathsf{emp},\top^{(\dsser{0})})
.
In the first case, we need to show
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:b\land\mathsf{emp}\rangle}~\mathsf{assume}~b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:b\land\mathsf{emp}\rangle}
. Suppose
m\vDash\mathsf{ok}:b\land\mathsf{emp}
, then it’s easy to see that
\left\llbracket\mathsf{assume}~b\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=m
, so the triple is valid. In the second case, we must show
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\mathsf{ok}:\lnot b\land\mathsf{emp}\rangle}~\mathsf{assume}~b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\langle\top^{(\dsser{0})}\rangle}
. Suppose
m\vDash\mathsf{ok}:\lnot b\land\mathsf{emp}
, so
\left\llbracket\mathsf{assume}~b\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{alloc}}\!\!(m)=\dsser{0}
, and
\dsser{0}\vDash\top^{(\dsser{0})}
.

\triangleright

C=\mathsf{assume}~a
. Any element of
\left\llbracket\mathsf{assume}~a\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}(T)
must have the form
(\mathsf{emp},(\mathsf{ok}:\mathsf{emp})^{(a)})
, so we need to show
